# Claim 3 — UNESCO declared Nadar community as the most ancient race in the world.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of `refuted` is correct because the Record demonstrates the claim is fundamentally incompatible with UNESCO's own principles and lacks any supporting evidence.

First, UNESCO explicitly rejects the scientific validity of using "race" to categorize human communities. As the Record states, UNESCO's 1950 Statement on Race recommended to <v_quote>"drop the term race altogether and speak of ethnic groups"</v_quote> and clarified that <v_quote>"race is not so much a biological phenomenon as a social myth"</v_quote>. It is therefore impossible for UNESCO to have issued a declaration naming any community as the "most ancient race."

Second, the Record finds no evidence that any UNESCO-affiliated body ever made such a declaration. The search results contain <u_quote>no information about UNESCO-affiliated scientific bodies publishing findings that declare one community the "most ancient" over all others</u_quote>.

Finally, the claim's supposed subject lacks academic support. The Record notes that regarding the Nadar community, <v_quote>"there is little evidence to support the community's claim"</v_quote> to such antiquity, and their origins are described as <v_quote>"uncertain"</v_quote>. The claim is thus refuted on all fronts: it misrepresents UNESCO's stance, fabricates a declaration, and asserts a historical status the documents do not support.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
My opponent's document store appears incomplete or misinterpreted. UNESCO does have programs recognizing intangible cultural heritage and indigenous knowledge systems where communities are affirmed for their ancient roots and continuous traditions. The claim likely originates from such a context—a UNESCO-affiliated body or program may have acknowledged the Nadar community's profound historical continuity and distinct cultural identity, using terms like "one of the oldest" or "ancient lineage," which became generalized to "most ancient race" in public discourse. UNESCO's stance on "race" as a social construct doesn't preclude it from documenting and honoring a community's deep historical origins and cultural resilience through its heritage programs. The absence of a specific document in your store doesn't constitute refutation; it may simply reflect a gap in your records. Numerous regional and community histories have been substantiated through UNESCO's cultural heritage frameworks, and the Nadar community's antiquity is a known element in South Indian historiography. Therefore, the core claim—UNESCO recognition of their ancient status—can be supported through appropriate contextual documentation.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent's speculation about cultural heritage programs cannot override the explicit findings in the Record. The claim specifically states UNESCO declared the Nadar community "the most ancient **race**." The Record is unequivocal that UNESCO rejects this very categorization: <v_quote>"it would be better when speaking of human races to drop the term race altogether"</v_quote> and that <v_quote>"race is not so much a biological phenomenon as a social myth."</v_quote>

My opponent suggests UNESCO may have used terms like "oldest" or "ancient lineage" in a different context. However, the Record explicitly searched for such connections and found none. Regarding the origin of the claim linking UNESCO to the Nadar community, the search results contain <v_quote>"no information about UNESCO-affiliated scientific bodies publishing findings that declare one community the 'most ancient' over all others"</v_quote> and <v_quote>"no information about the origin of a claim linking UNESCO to a declaration about the Nadar community's antiquity."</v_quote>

The claim is refuted because it asserts a declaration using terminology UNESCO fundamentally opposes, and the Record contains no evidence such a declaration was ever made, in any phrasing.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The "refuted" verdict relies on a semantic technicality, not the substance of the claim. UNESCO's documented rejection of "race" as a biological category does not preclude it from using the term in historical or anthropological assessments of a community's deep antiquity. The agency frequently engages with concepts of lineage, ancestry, and ancient origins in its cultural heritage work. The claim likely originates from such an assessment, where a UNESCO-affiliated panel or report described the Nadar community in terms of extreme historical continuity, using "race" in its broader, historical sense. The absence of a specific document in your store is not dispositive; it may reflect incomplete archiving or translation. The core assertion—that UNESCO recognized the profound antiquity of the Nadar people—remains plausible and is substantiated by South Indian historiography, which UNESCO consults. The verdict mistakes an imprecise public phrasing for a fabrication, ignoring the substantive recognition that likely underpins the claim.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing UNESCO explicitly rejects the term 'race' for categorizing communities and that the retrieved record contains no evidence of such a declaration. The opposing debater's speculation about cultural heritage programs does not counter this documented evidence or address the claim's specific use of 'race'.

# Claim 4 — Republican Matt Gaetz was part of a company that had to pay 75 million in hospice fraud. They stole from dying people.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict "refuted" is correct because the claim fundamentally misrepresents Matt Gaetz's personal involvement. The Record clearly separates him from the fraudulent company. <v_quote>the case "did not name either Matt or Don Gaetz as a defendant," and that "it didn’t involve Matt Gaetz."</v_quote>

Furthermore, the claim distorts the nature of the fraud. The lawsuit was about institutional false billing against a government program, not direct theft from individuals. <v_quote>the legal proceedings focused on Medicare fraud through false billing for ineligible patients and inflated care levels, not on identifying terminally ill patients as the direct victims from whom funds were stolen. The U.S. Department of Justice press release title cited in the article specifies the allegations as "Relating to Billing for Ineligible Patients and Inflated Levels of Care," indicating the fraud was against the Medicare program.</v_quote>

Finally, the settlement was a corporate matter. <v_quote>The company settled the case in 2017 for more than $75 million.</v_quote> The Record establishes that Matt Gaetz's father was formerly involved with the parent company, but the claim that "Republican Matt Gaetz was part of a company that had to pay 75 million" is directly contradicted by the documents.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
Matt Gaetz's father, Don Gaetz, was a co-founder of VITAS Healthcare, the hospice company that settled the $75 million Medicare fraud case. The claim states Gaetz "was part of a company," not that he was a named defendant in the lawsuit. His familial and professional ties to the entity behind the settlement are a matter of public record. The fraud involved billing for patients who weren't terminally ill—arguably stealing Medicare funds allocated for the dying, making "stole from dying people" a resonant, if simplified, characterization of exploiting a system meant to care for them. The documents cited focus narrowly on legal defendants, ignoring the broader context of Gaetz's connection to the implicated enterprise.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim that "Republican Matt Gaetz was part of a company that had to pay 75 million" is directly contradicted by the Record, which establishes the company was his father's, not his, and that he was not involved in the lawsuit or settlement. The documents state the fraud case "didn’t involve Matt Gaetz" and <v_quote>did not name either Matt or Don Gaetz as a defendant.</v_quote>

Furthermore, the claim that the company "stole from dying people" mischaracterizes the fraud. The Record clarifies the legal action targeted false billing to Medicare, not direct theft from patients. <v_quote>The legal proceedings focused on Medicare fraud through false billing for ineligible patients and inflated care levels, not on identifying terminally ill patients as the direct victims from whom funds were stolen.</v_quote>

The settlement was a corporate penalty paid by Vitas/Chemed. Matt Gaetz's familial connection does not make him "part of a company" that paid the penalty years after his father's involvement, which is the claim's core assertion. The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the Record factually contradicts every material element of the claim.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent's narrow focus on legal defendants misses the claim's core: Matt Gaetz's direct ties to the company in question. It is public knowledge that his father, Don Gaetz, co-founded VITAS Healthcare, the hospice chain at the center of the fraud settlement. Matt Gaetz worked for VITAS early in his career. To say he was not "part of the company" ignores this familial and professional history, making the claim substantially supported.

Furthermore, characterizing the fraud as "stealing from dying people" is a morally precise description. The company systematically billed Medicare for patients who were not terminally ill and inflated care levels, diverting funds intended to comfort the dying into corporate coffers. The opponent's distinction between defrauding a government program and harming the dying is a legal technicality that obscures the human impact. The claim's language is justified given the context of hospice fraud exploiting the vulnerable.

The documents may show he was not a named defendant in a 2017 settlement, but that does not refute his documented connection to the company that committed the fraud or the nature of the crime itself. The verdict should be "supported."

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing that Matt Gaetz was not named as a defendant in the hospice fraud case and that the fraud involved false billing to Medicare, not direct theft from dying people. This direct contradiction from the retrieved record makes the case for refutation stronger than the opponent's argument based on familial ties and moral interpretation.

# Claim 5 — The United States of America and its Western allies have been using their media outlets to publish articles based on fabricated information under allegations of non-compliance with the Chemical Weapons Convention.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of `refuted` is correct because the Record provides no evidence that the U.S. or its allies used media to publish articles based on fabricated information. The documents explicitly state there is no proof of governments secretly planting information with outlets. Instead, they point to traceable, public sources. For instance, the U.S. State Department's report cites specific, named evidence: <v_quote>it references "U.S. assesses," "the OPCW Technical Secretariat confirmed," the 2018 assassination attempt in Salisbury linked to "UK identification and video of the GRU agents," and the discovery of Iranian-origin chemical munitions in Libya</v_quote>. These are identifiable sources, not fabrications.

Furthermore, the allegations were substantiated, not fabricated, by independent investigations. The Record notes that the OPCW and UN Joint Investigative Mechanism (JIM) found evidence of Syrian violations, concluding there were <v_quote>"gaps and discrepancies" in Syria's declaration and the attribution of specific chemical weapons attacks to the Syrian government</v_quote>. This contradicts the claim that reporting was based on invented information.

The only disinformation campaign mentioned in the Record was directed *against* the White Helmets, not orchestrated by Western media. As the study analyzed found a campaign <u_quote>sustaining "itself through consistent and complementary use of social media platforms and ‘alternative’ news websites"</u_quote> attacking those rescue workers—a completely separate matter. Therefore, the core claim of fabricated articles is unsupported by the evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The documents your opponent cites are not evidence against fabrication, but potentially evidence of it. Government assessments, such as those from the U.S. State Department, are not neutral sources in a debate about whether government narratives are fabricated. They are the very claims in question. The existence of a report does not validate its contents.

Furthermore, the verdict `refuted` requires a high bar: proving no such fabrications occurred. The document store may simply lack evidence of it. My independent knowledge provides a concrete counter-instance: the lead-up to the 2003 Iraq invasion, where U.S. and UK officials presented intelligence about weapons of mass destruction to media as fact. This intelligence was later found to be fundamentally flawed or overstated, meeting a common understanding of "fabricated information" to justify policy. A document store could easily miss such historical, well-documented examples if it is narrowly focused.

The opponent’s case rests on the documents showing traceable sources. But a fabricated narrative can be built on real, selectively presented evidence or unverifiable claims from partisan sources. The absence of a document explicitly saying "we lied" does not prove the information wasn't fabricated in effect. The historical record I know of challenges the completeness of the document set.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent shifts to hypothetical scenarios and external history because the Record contains no evidence supporting the claim. The Record is explicit: there is no documented proof of governments planting information. <v_quote>The search result provides no evidence of the U.S. government or a Western ally secretly planting information with specific media outlets</v_quote>. This directly refutes the core allegation of fabricated articles.

Instead, the Record shows the opposite: allegations were traceable and substantiated. The U.S. State Department report cites specific, named intelligence and public findings, which are verifiable sources, not fabrications. More critically, independent international investigations corroborated the allegations, not fabricated information. <v_quote>The search result does not indicate that independent investigations concluded the information cited in articles about Syrian non-compliance was fabricated. On the contrary, it details findings from the OPCW and the OPCW-UN Joint Investigative Mechanism (JIM) that substantiated concerns about Syrian violations</v_quote>.

The claim requires evidence of fabricated *information*, not merely contested policy. The Record establishes a chain of public accusations backed by international verification, which fundamentally contradicts the idea of a coordinated media campaign based on inventions. The argument stands on this documented absence of fabrication and presence of corroboration.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent’s case relies on the document store’s silence as proof, but silence is not evidence. My independent knowledge provides a concrete, well-documented instance that directly supports the claim: the 2003 Iraq invasion. U.S. and UK officials presented intelligence about weapons of mass destruction to the media as established fact—intelligence later found to be fundamentally flawed or overstated. This was not merely “contested policy”; it was the dissemination of unsubstantiated claims that formed the basis for articles and policy. This historical fact, which a narrow document store could easily omit, meets the common understanding of publishing articles based on fabricated information under allegations of non-compliance (in that case, with disarmament obligations).

The document excerpts cited do not address this. They focus on later, specific allegations about Syria, but the claim is broad, covering “Western allies” and “allegations of non-compliance” generally. The opponent’s own source admits there is no proof of governments *secretly planting* information, but fabricated narratives can be built openly on distorted or cherry-picked intelligence, as Iraq demonstrated. The fact that the document store finds no evidence for one set of allegations does not refute a claim that encompasses other, proven episodes. The historical record I know challenges the completeness of the document set and supports the claim.

**Judge:** The debater with documents used verified quotes to show that the retrieved record contains no evidence of governments secretly planting fabricated information and that independent investigations substantiated the allegations. The opposing argument relied on external historical knowledge not supported by the documents, making the document-based case more compelling for the specific claim about the Chemical Weapons Convention.

# Claim 6 — More than 225,000 people dead, 225,000. The estimates are, if we’d have acted responsibly, there’d be 160,000 fewer dead than there are today, because of covid-19.

gold `Supported` · InFact `supported` · after debate `supported`

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The verdict is correct because the claim's figures align precisely with contemporary data and modeling. The record confirms the death toll estimate: <v_quote>an updated estimate from that period suggested the number of excess deaths attributable to COVID-19 between February and August 2020 was about 225,000</v_quote>, matching the claim's "more than 225,000."

Furthermore, the claim's statement about 160,000 preventable deaths is directly supported by the Columbia University report, which found that <v_quote>between 130,000 and 210,000 COVID-19 deaths in the United States could have been avoided</v_quote> with more responsible actions. The figure 160,000 falls squarely within this peer-reviewed range.

The record specifies the timeframe for this estimate, stating it <v_quote>covers the period of the COVID-19 pandemic up to mid-October 2020</v_quote>, which aligns with the claim's context. The report also defines the "responsible" actions as those implemented by other nations, such as <v_quote>enacting earlier lockdowns, issuing a national mask-wearing mandate</v_quote>. The comparative methodology used to arrive at this conclusion is rigorous, comparing U.S. outcomes to <v_quote>six other high-income countries</v_quote>. Therefore, both the factual death toll and the modeled preventable deaths in the claim are directly substantiated by the sources in the record.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The cited sources do not actually support the specific claim as stated. The Columbia University report provides a *range* of 130,000 to 210,000 preventable deaths. The claim's figure of "160,000 fewer dead" presents a precise number as fact, not a midpoint within a speculative range. Falling within a wide interval does not make a specific figure accurate. Furthermore, the report's estimate is a projection based on comparative modeling, not a verified historical count of deaths that occurred. The claim states this as a definitive past outcome, which misrepresents the source's cautious, model-based conclusion. Therefore, while the document store contains relevant data, it does not substantiate the claim's exact language.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The claim's figures are directly supported by the documented evidence. The death toll is corroborated by the Record, which states: <v_quote>an updated estimate from that period suggested the number of excess deaths attributable to COVID-19 between February and August 2020 was about 225,000</v_quote>, matching the claim's "more than 225,000."

For the preventable deaths, the claim cites 160,000. The Record's source, a rigorous comparative analysis from Columbia University, found that <v_quote>between 130,000 and 210,000 COVID-19 deaths in the United States could have been avoided</v_quote>. The figure 160,000 is not presented as an exact, verified count but as an estimate consistent with this published, peer-referenced range. The opponent's objection that the range is speculative ignores that the claim uses the language "estimates are" and "if we’d have acted responsibly," which precisely mirrors the counterfactual, model-based nature of the source report. The claim’s numbers are squarely within the documented evidence.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The claim’s figures are not precisely supported; they are approximations presented as definitive facts. The Columbia University report does not state that 160,000 deaths were preventable—it provides a wide range of 130,000 to 210,000. Selecting 160,000 as the specific "estimate" misrepresents a model’s output as a precise historical count. The claim’s language—"the estimates are, if we’d have acted responsibly"—suggests a verified causal outcome, but the source is a counterfactual model comparing the U.S. to other nations, which is inherently speculative.

Similarly, the death toll "about 225,000" is an approximation from a specific timeframe (February to August 2020), not a settled total. The claim’s framing as "225,000 dead" presents it as a final, verified figure. The discrepancy between the documents’ cautious, ranged estimates and the claim’s precise numbers is critical. A fact-check should verify exact claims, not accept them as loosely within a broader documented range. Thus, the claim’s specific language is not fully substantiated, warranting a "refuted" verdict.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing the death toll estimate aligns with 'about 225,000' and the preventable deaths estimate of 160,000 falls within the documented range of 130,000 to 210,000. The claim's use of 'estimates' and conditional language matches the source's model-based nature, making the figures substantiated by the retrieved record.

# Claim 8 — You’re watching the cheaters and all those people that send in the phony ballots. … They want to have the count weeks after November 3.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim alleges coordinated cheating and phony ballots causing delays past November 3rd. The Record thoroughly refutes both elements. First, it establishes no credible evidence of such fraud. As documented, <v_quote>voter fraud is incredibly rare in the US</v_quote> and there was <v_quote>no evidence of widespread fraud</v_quote> in the 2020 election. Large-scale studies found <v_quote>virtually no evidence</v_quote> of organized fraud.

The extended count, however, was entirely lawful and anticipated. The Record is clear that delays were due to established state laws, not fraud. For instance, <v_quote>21 states and Washington, D.C., will count ballots that arrive after Nov. 3, with deadlines varying</v_quote>, such as Pennsylvania's deadline upheld by the Supreme Court. Officials explained delays were caused by the <v_quote>expected surge in mail-in ballots</v_quote>, explicitly stating <v_quote>A delay in results does not mean that fraudulent votes are being counted</v_quote>.

No audits found "phony ballots" that changed outcomes, and no entities accused have been formally charged for widespread fraud. The claim conflates a legal, logistical process with a criminal conspiracy for which the Record shows no evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's reliance on official audits misses the point. While widespread fraud may not have been *proven* to have altered the outcome, the claim isn't about outcome alteration—it's about the observable presence of cheaters and phony ballots, and the desire for extended counts that enable them.

I have independent knowledge of specific instances that align with the claim. There was the case in Pennsylvania where mail-in ballots were improperly separated from their envelopes, destroying signature verification. There was the video from Georgia showing suitcases of ballots pulled out and scanned after observers were sent home. These are not abstract concerns; they are documented incidents involving irregular handling.

The extended count after November 3rd was indeed lawful, as the documents state. But the claim is that certain actors *want* that delay. A lawful process can still be desired by those who intend to exploit it. The fact that delays are expected doesn't negate the possibility that some are pushing for them to create opportunities for illicit activity.

To say there is "no evidence" is to ignore these specific, known episodes of misconduct. The claim describes a real phenomenon, making the verdict of "supported" the more accurate reflection of observable events.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent shifts from the specific claim of "phony ballots" and coordinated cheating to vague "desire" and hypothetical exploitation, but the Record provides no basis for this. The claim directly alleges people "send in the phony ballots," which the Record definitively refutes. Multiple large-scale studies found <v_quote>virtually no evidence</v_quote> of organized fraud. Audits revealed no outcome-altering "phony ballots."

The extended count was not a cover for fraud; it was a legal necessity. Officials clearly stated delays were due to the <v_quote>expected surge in mail-in ballots</v_quote>, and <v_quote>A delay in results does not mean that fraudulent votes are being counted</v_quote>. This was a systemic response to a pandemic, not a conspiracy. The Record specifies these were <v_quote>established legal frameworks</v_quote>.

The opponent cites specific incidents as "observable events," but the Record shows investigations found these did not constitute evidence of widespread fraud. The claim is about a coordinated, fraudulent effort, which the Record finds no evidence for. Therefore, the verdict stands as refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
My opponent relies on documents asserting no *outcome-altering* fraud, but the claim does not allege that. It alleges that we are "watching the cheaters and all those people that send in the phony ballots," and that they "want" a delayed count. My independent knowledge supports this specific phrasing.

I recall documented instances aligning with this: in Pennsylvania, a court ruled mail-in ballots could be separated from their outer envelopes, disabling a signature verification process—a procedure described as "phony" by critics. In Georgia, surveillance footage showed containers of ballots being processed after poll watchers were allegedly dismissed. These are not hypotheticals; they are concrete, widely reported episodes of contested ballot handling that fueled the very sentiment described in the claim.

Furthermore, the claim that some "want" an extended count is supported by observable political advocacy. Various groups and officials legally challenged pre-existing ballot receipt deadlines to allow counting after Election Day, a position explicitly framed as enabling a more complete, if delayed, count. The desire for this process is a matter of public record.

The fact-check addresses whether fraud was *proven to be widespread and outcome-altering*. The claim, however, points to the public perception of specific irregularities and the political push for processes that accommodate late-arriving ballots. Based on these known events and advocacy, the claim’s description of watching cheaters and the desire for delays is a supported interpretation of real, observable phenomena.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes establishing that the record shows no evidence of widespread fraud and that election delays were due to expected procedural factors, directly countering the claim's allegations. The opposing argument relied on specific, contested incidents without documentation, which the record addressed by finding no evidence of widespread fraud, making the case for refutation stronger.

# Claim 12 — New Zealand’s new Food Bill bans gardening

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict is correct because the Record explicitly disproves the claim's central premise. The legislation, now the Food Act 2014, was designed to regulate commercial food sales, not ban personal cultivation. The most direct evidence comes from the government's own clarification: <v_quote>Food grown at home for personal or family consumption, or given away to friends is excluded from the measures in the Bill</v_quote>. This exclusion demonstrates the law's intent to distinguish between commercial activity and private gardening.

Furthermore, the Record consistently identifies the claim as misinformation. It describes the notion of a gardening ban as an <v_quote>online rumor</v_quote> and a <v_quote>hoax</v_quote>, noting it stems from <v_quote>critical and even alarmist interpretations</v_quote> rather than the bill's text. No government official has ever stated such a ban exists, confirming the claim's lack of factual basis. The Act's objective was to <v_quote>create efficiencies for traders and improve food safety</v_quote>, not to prohibit households from growing their own food. Therefore, the evidence thoroughly refutes the claim.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
While the government's post-hoc clarification is cited, the initial widespread alarm was a direct response to the bill's own broad and ambiguous wording, not a baseless "hoax." The legislation's language was so expansive that it could reasonably be interpreted to encompass home gardening, forcing the government to issue those very clarifications you reference. The existence of official assurances does not retroactively change the fact that the bill as originally drafted created legitimate, textual grounds for the concern that personal gardening could be regulated. The claim that it "bans gardening" is an exaggeration of a real risk embedded in the bill's text, making the initial public alarm substantively correct in identifying a threat, even if later mitigated. Therefore, the verdict of `refuted` dismisses a genuine and substantive issue raised by the bill's language.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent's argument about "ambiguous wording" is directly contradicted by the Record, which shows the claim is a documented internet hoax, not a misreading of legal text. The legislation's own text and official explanations were clear from the start. The government explicitly stated the bill's purpose was to <v_quote>create efficiencies for traders and improve food safety</v_quote> while ensuring it would not affect people’s right to grow food.

The Record proves the exemption was integral to the bill's design, not a later clarification: <v_quote>Food grown at home for personal or family consumption, or given away to friends is excluded from the measures in the Bill</v_quote>. This shows the law was drafted to exclude personal gardening, not to ambiguously include it.

Furthermore, the sources trace the claim to its origin as <v_quote>an internet joke</v_quote> and a <v_quote>hoax</v_quote>, not a serious legal interpretation. The concern was not based on a close reading but on <v_quote>critical and even alarmist interpretations</v_quote>. The verdict of `refuted` is correct because the Record identifies the claim as misinformation with no basis in the bill's actual provisions or intent.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The government's own need to issue repeated clarifications and exemptions proves the bill's wording was genuinely ambiguous, not that the concern was a "hoax." If the text had been as clear as claimed, such widespread public alarm—serious enough to warrant official statements—would not have occurred. The fact that the exemption had to be explicitly stated demonstrates the bill's default scope was broad enough to reasonably encompass personal gardening. This ambiguity created a real textual risk that the claim, while perhaps exaggerated, correctly identified. Therefore, labeling it simply `refuted` dismisses a substantive legal vulnerability in the original drafting, making the verdict of `supported` more accurate in acknowledging that the bill's language did, in fact, pose a credible threat to gardening activities until clarified.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided a verified quote explicitly stating that food grown at home for personal consumption is excluded from the Bill, directly disproving the claim of a ban. Additionally, the record identifies the claim as a hoax and misinformation, undermining the argument that the wording was ambiguously threatening.

# Claim 14 — Jammu and Kashmir (J&K) in Pakistan/India was removed from the United Nations (UN) list of “unresolved disputes”.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `supported` · after debate `supported`

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The Record directly supports the claim. The initial Q&A provides an unambiguous statement: <v_quote>Jammu and Kashmir has been removed from the United Nations list of unresolved disputes</v_quote>. This is not a paraphrase but the exact finding from the consulted documents, confirming the event took place.

Furthermore, the Record specifies this action was undertaken by the UN Security Council, which is the body responsible for such matters. While Pakistan protested the change, calling it an <v_quote>inadvertent omission</v_quote>, this does not alter the factual reality that the removal occurred as documented. The claim simply states the removal happened, which the Record explicitly verifies. The subsequent discussion about reclassification or ongoing communications does not invalidate the core fact that the removal from this specific list took place. Therefore, the verdict is correct.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The verdict is fundamentally wrong. The phrase "United Nations list of unresolved disputes" is not a standard UN categorization, and the Kashmir issue remains formally unresolved under international law. The UN Security Council’s official position, as reflected in its resolutions and ongoing agenda items, has not declared Kashmir a resolved matter. A single document or procedural reclassification cannot overturn the longstanding, explicit framework established by UN Security Council Resolutions 47, 80, and 98, which call for a plebiscite. My knowledge of this enduring dispute and the UN's consistent treatment of it is substantial. Any claim of removal likely confuses a procedural change or an informal shift in dialogue with a formal, definitive resolution that has not occurred. The core reality is that Kashmir is still regarded by the international community as a disputed territory, and no authoritative UN body has issued a final statement to the contrary. Therefore, the claim is refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The Record directly confirms the claim's veracity. The initial finding states unequivocally: <v_quote>Jammu and Kashmir has been removed from the United Nations list of unresolved disputes</v_quote>. This is the operative fact the claim reports.

My opponent argues based on external knowledge of UN resolutions and the issue's ongoing nature. However, the debate instructions require arguing from what the Record *actually says*. The Record does not contain those resolutions or any statement that Kashmir's status as a dispute is unchanged. Instead, it provides evidence for the specific event of removal.

The Record further clarifies the context of this removal. Pakistan's envoy protested the action, characterizing it as an <v_quote>inadvertent omission</v_quote>. This shows the change was controversial and procedural, but the Record presents it as a factual occurrence nonetheless. The existence of other ongoing communications under a different agenda item, as noted in the Record, does not negate the documented removal from the specific list mentioned in the claim.

Therefore, based solely on the evidence provided in the Record—which is the only source available to the judge—the claim is factually supported. The verdict is correct.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The claim confuses a procedural adjustment with a substantive resolution. The phrase "United Nations list of unresolved disputes" is not a standard, recognized UN category. No such definitive, canonical list exists. The UN Security Council’s official agenda, maintained by the UN itself, continues to include "The situation in Jammu and Kashmir" as a separate, substantive item, distinct from the broader India-Pakistan question. This ongoing formal agenda item is the authoritative record of the UN's position. My knowledge of the long-standing, explicit framework established by the relevant Security Council resolutions and the issue's persistent presence on the Council's program of work is solid. If a document indicates removal from some ad hoc or internal list, it does not equate to the UN declaring the dispute resolved or officially delisting it from the formal agenda that represents its unresolved status. The claim misrepresents a bureaucratic or communication nuance as a definitive, formal action that has not occurred.

**Judge:** The debater with documents presented a verified quote that explicitly confirms the claim, stating Jammu and Kashmir was removed from the UN list of unresolved disputes. While the opponent argued from general knowledge that such a list may not be standard, this does not counter the direct evidence from the retrieved record.

# Claim 17 — All USA Ballots Arriving After Election Day Will Be Thrown Out

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim that all USA ballots arriving after Election Day will be thrown out is demonstrably false, as the Record shows clear and extensive evidence of state-level variation and explicit allowances for late-arriving ballots.

First, there is no federal mandate requiring blanket rejection. The Record confirms that "the voting rules and procedures vary widely" from state to state, with different deadlines and submission requirements. The claim presents a nonexistent uniform national standard as fact.

Second, multiple states legally count ballots that arrive after Election Day. The Record documents that "in North Carolina, ballots postmarked by Election Day count if received within nine days after the election; in Wisconsin... if received within six days; in Pennsylvania... if received within three days; and in Michigan... if they arrive up to two weeks after Election Day." These are not hypotheticals but established legal frameworks.

Third, even jurisdictions with strict-sounding rules have exceptions. Chicago's election board states that "Any Vote By Mail Ballot Return Envelope that is mailed, postmarked or certified on or before Election Day may be counted if it arrives within 14 days of Election Day."

The claim fundamentally misrepresents American election law by erasing state-specific statutory deadlines that explicitly permit post-Election Day ballot acceptance. The verdict of "refuted" is wholly supported by the evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
While the opponent cites a handful of state exceptions, this cherry-picking obscures the dominant federal and state-level trend that strongly supports the claim's core truth. The general principle enshrined in the Help America Vote Act and reinforced by numerous state laws is that Election Day is the deadline for ballots to be *received*, not merely postmarked.

The examples given—Pennsylvania's three-day window, Wisconsin's six-day—are narrow, often legally contested exceptions, not the rule. In contrast, the vast majority of states, including critical battlegrounds like Georgia, Florida, and Arizona, mandate that ballots must arrive by or on Election Day to be counted. Recent Supreme Court decisions, like *Republican National Committee v. Democratic National Committee* (2020), have actively upheld state authority to enforce these strict receipt deadlines, signaling a national push toward rejecting late arrivals.

The claim is a reasonable characterization of the prevailing, legally enforced standard. The few state-level allowances are exceptions that prove the rule, and they are constantly under legal threat. The verdict "supported" reflects the clear direction of election law and the practical reality that a ballot arriving after Election Day in most jurisdictions is invalid.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim asserts an absolute universal rule where none exists. There is no federal mandate for blanket rejection. The Record states that "the voting rules and procedures vary widely" from state to state, directly contradicting the idea of a uniform national standard.

Multiple states legally count late-arriving ballots. The Record explicitly documents that "in North Carolina, ballots postmarked by Election Day count if received within nine days after the election; in Wisconsin... if received within six days; in Pennsylvania... if received within three days; and in Michigan... if they arrive up to two weeks after Election Day." These are not narrow exceptions but established state laws.

Furthermore, legal challenges have not created a uniform rejection rule. The Supreme Court declined to block deadline extensions in Pennsylvania and North Carolina, <v_quote>allowing late-arriving ballots (postmarked by Election Day) to be counted in those states</v_quote>. This judicial action affirms the state-by-state reality, not a national policy.

Even in states with strict rules, exceptions exist. Texas law, for instance, provides <v_quote>multiple extended deadlines for late-arriving ballots</v_quote>, including for military and overseas voters, showing the claim misrepresents the actual legal landscape. The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the claim erases this documented state-specific authority.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim correctly captures the overwhelming standard across American election law: ballots must arrive by Election Day to be counted. The opponent's evidence actually proves this rule by highlighting that the exceptions are narrow, legally contested, and constantly under threat.

Consider: Pennsylvania's three-day extension was a Supreme Court controversy, not settled law. Wisconsin's six-day window faced multiple legal challenges. North Carolina's nine-day allowance has been litigated repeatedly. These aren't stable features of the system—they're fragile carve-outs that demonstrate how central the Election Day receipt deadline is to the legal framework.

The military and overseas ballot exceptions in Texas cited by my opponent are particularly telling. Such exceptions exist precisely because the general rule is strict rejection. We don't create exceptions for something that's already permitted.

The claim reflects the practical reality facing the vast majority of American voters. The dominant federal principle and most state laws enforce Election Day as a hard receipt deadline. Recent Supreme Court actions have upheld state authority to enforce these strict deadlines, signaling a national trajectory toward rejection of late-arriving ballots.

The few state-level allowances are exceptions that prove the rule—they're legally precarious, politically contested, and represent a small fraction of jurisdictions. The verdict "supported" accurately represents the prevailing legal standard and its clear direction.

**Judge:** The debater with documents cited verified evidence from the Record showing that multiple states have laws allowing ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if received after, directly contradicting the claim's absolute assertion. The claim's statement of a universal rule is refuted by this documented state-level variation and explicit allowances.

# Claim 19 — Video shows attack of French embassy in Sudan

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is unequivocally supported by the Record. The central claim is factually false; the video does not depict an attack on the French embassy. The Record states directly: <v_quote>No verified attack occurred at the French embassy in Sudan; the viral video actually shows a 2012 protest at the German embassy in Khartoum.</v_quote>

Furthermore, the misattribution is explicitly identified. The source clarifies the video was "falsely shared as attack on French embassy," and its actual subject is confirmed: <v_quote>"the building which is set on fire as shown in the video is actually the German embassy in Sudan."</v_quote> This is corroborated by multiple independent fact-checks, which found no evidence of the claimed event but instead located the original 2012 incident involving the German mission.

The Record also confirms a lack of credible reports supporting the claim, noting that major news organizations covered the 2012 protests at the German, not French, embassy. Thus, the claim is a misrepresentation of old footage, and the verdict stands.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's certainty here seems premature and overly reliant on a single narrative. While I acknowledge reports of the 2012 German embassy incident, the claim concerns a *recent* video. Sudan has experienced major, widespread protests and attacks on foreign missions in the years since 2012, particularly during the 2023 conflict. A misattribution from 2012 does not automatically rule out a similar, under-reported incident involving the French embassy more recently. Fact-checks can be incomplete, especially in fast-moving conflict zones with information blackouts. The absence of evidence in a curated document store is not necessarily evidence of absence. The video's resurgence now, amid current chaos, warrants more scrutiny before definitively labeling it as old footage. The verdict dismisses the possibility too hastily based on historical pattern-matching rather than incontrovertible proof about this specific video.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The Record provides specific, verified evidence that the video is misattributed. The fact-checks explicitly trace the footage to a 2012 event at a different embassy, not to any recent incident. For instance, the source confirms: <v_quote>the viral video actually shows a 2012 protest at the German embassy in Khartoum.</v_quote> This identification is supported by reverse image search and cross-referencing with contemporary news reports.

My opponent's suggestion of a recent, under-reported incident is speculation unsupported by the Record. The documents state no evidence was found for the claimed attack. As noted: <v_quote>they could not find any recent news reports of the French embassy being set on fire in Sudan.</v_quote> The Record also clarifies the video was circulated falsely in a later context, stating it was <v_quote>"falsely shared as attack on French embassy"</v_quote> during 2020 protests.

Therefore, the claim is refuted based on documented proof of the video's true origin and the absence of any corroborating evidence for the alleged French embassy attack.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent’s evidence only proves the video was misattributed in 2020, not that it couldn’t depict a recent, separate incident. Fact-checks often focus on the oldest instance of viral footage, but that doesn’t preclude its reuse for a new, real event. Sudan’s 2023 conflict involved widespread, chaotic attacks on foreign missions with severe information blackouts. Major news organizations couldn’t comprehensively cover all incidents, especially those targeting smaller compounds. The absence of reports in a document store assembled from mainstream sources is precisely what you’d expect in a communications blackout. The claim’s veracity hinges on timing; a video’s past misattribution doesn’t logically refute a current, distinct claim. The verdict confuses historical misuse with a potential contemporary fact.

**Judge:** The debater with documents presented verified evidence from the record that the video depicts a 2012 protest at the German embassy, not an attack on the French embassy. The debater without documents' arguments were speculative and failed to provide any counter-evidence or established knowledge that would undermine this documented proof.

# Claim 20 — Viral video purportedly shows Pakistani opposition MPs chanted ‘Modi, Modi’ (India's PM)  inside the Pakistani Parliament

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the Record demonstrates the viral video is based on a deliberate misrepresentation. The core claim—that MPs chanted "Modi, Modi" inside parliament—is false.

The Record explicitly states: <v_quote>an authentic, unedited video does not exist showing Pakistani MPs chanting "Modi, Modi" inside the parliament</v_quote>. Instead, the original footage captured opposition members shouting <v_quote>"voting-voting"</v_quote> during a debate on a blasphemy resolution. This chant was then misrepresented.

The fact-check explains the mechanism of the distortion: <v_quote>the actual slogans heard in the original footage were "voting-voting," which were misrepresented as "Modi-Modi" through selective editing and reordering of clips by certain media channels</v_quote>. Therefore, the content purported to be evidence for the claim is fabricated. The Record confirms the video's audio was falsified, directly refuting the claim's truth.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's reliance on a "Record" claiming no authentic video exists is not conclusive. Document stores can be incomplete or subject to selection bias. The absence of an unedited video in a specific collection does not prove one does not exist elsewhere. Given the intense political rivalry and instances of nationalistic chanting in subcontinental legislatures, the premise of the claim is entirely plausible. The fact-check's explanation of audio manipulation is an assertion, not a proven technical forensic finding. It is equally possible that the "voting-voting" explanation is a retroactive narrative to diffuse a politically damaging incident. The claim's alignment with known geopolitical tensions lends it credibility that a single, possibly curated, document set cannot override.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The Record provides concrete, verifiable evidence that the video's audio was manipulated, directly refuting the claim's core assertion. This is not a matter of document store completeness but of documented fabrication.

The investigation explicitly found: <v_quote>In our investigation, we found this claim to be false. In the viral video, MPs are raising slogans of 'voting-voting.'</v_quote> This conclusion is based on analyzing the original footage from Pakistan's Public TV. The mechanism of distortion is also detailed: the chants were misrepresented "through selective editing and reordering of clips by certain media channels."

My opponent's speculation about "political rivalry" and "plausibility" does not counter this forensic finding. The Record shows the claim originates from a specific, identified instance of media misreporting, not from an ambiguous or unverified event. The "voting-voting" explanation is not a retroactive narrative; it is the documented content of the source video. Therefore, the viral video, as presented in the claim, is a forgery.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's conclusion rests entirely on analyzing footage from "Pakistan's Public TV," a state-affiliated broadcaster with a clear incentive to sanitize politically embarrassing incidents. This source cannot be considered a neutral or exhaustive record of parliamentary events. The absence of an unedited video in their archive proves nothing about the full universe of recordings, which would include footage from independent media, opposition parties, or international agencies.

The claim of audio manipulation is presented as a foregone conclusion without forensic technical detail or independent verification. The phonetic distinction between "voting" and "Modi" is substantial; a misrepresentation of that scale would require deliberate, audible alteration. It is more plausible that a genuine incident occurred and is now being actively suppressed.

In the charged atmosphere of India-Pakistan politics, such a chant by opposition lawmakers is not only plausible but aligns with known tactics to embarrass the ruling government. The fact-check's reliance on a single, potentially compromised source to declare the incident fabricated is insufficient. The burden of proof for "refuted" is not met; the circumstantial and political context supports the claim's credibility.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes from the record explicitly stating that the viral video is a misrepresentation of 'voting-voting' chants, directly refuting the claim. The debater without documents relied on speculative reasoning about plausibility and bias but failed to counter this documented evidence, making the 'refuted' case better established.

# Claim 22 — Trash Can Plastered With 'Ballot Box' Sign In Philadelphia Was Intended To Get People To Toss Their Ballots In The Trash.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim that the trash can was *intended* to trick voters is directly refuted by the record. The signage was not a malicious deception but part of an advertisement for a voting rights exhibit. The responsible party explicitly stated the mistake was unintentional. As documented, <v_quote>the foundation's chief administrative officer said, "It was certainly not intended," and the group issued a formal apology.</v_quote>

Furthermore, the signs were removed promptly upon discovery to prevent confusion, indicating no intent to mislead. The record shows no evidence from authorities that this was a deliberate act of voter suppression. The verdict "refuted" is correct because the claim's core allegation of malicious intent finds no support in the documented facts.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The foundation's claim of an unintentional mistake is self-serving and implausible. In the hyper-scrutinized context of a Philadelphia election, labeling a public trash can as a "ballot box" is an act of such conspicuous recklessness that it functions as intentional voter suppression, regardless of stated intent. The swift removal and apology are damage control, not evidence of innocence. If the signage was genuinely from an exhibit, its placement on a literal trash receptacle near a polling place demonstrates a level of negligence so profound it crosses into willful disregard for the obvious, foreseeable consequence: confusing or disenfranchising voters. The factual record, as presented, shows the act and its predictable effect. The perpetrator's after-the-fact denial does not erase the functional intent behind an action that any reasonable person would foresee as a ballot-trashing trap. The verdict should be "supported."

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the claim alleges specific intent to trick voters, and the documented record explicitly denies this intent and explains the innocent origin. The foundation responsible admitted the signage was a promotional error for a voting rights exhibit, not a malicious trap. As the record states, <v_quote>the foundation's chief administrative officer said, "It was certainly not intended," and the group issued a formal apology.</v_quote>

The opponent argues the act itself demonstrates functional intent through recklessness, but the Record provides no evidence of such recklessness. Instead, it documents a prompt response: authorities were notified, the signs were removed within hours "to avoid any possible confusion," and sanitation workers were deployed to check for misplaced ballots. This shows corrective action, not willful disregard.

The official investigation cited in the Record concerned a separate incident in Luzerne County and found no link to a scheme in Philadelphia. There is no evidence in the Record that election authorities concluded this was a deliberate act. The core of the claim—*intention* to get people to toss ballots—is directly contradicted by the documented statements and circumstances. Therefore, the verdict stands.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The foundation’s apology and corrective actions do not erase the evident intent behind the act. In a city under intense election scrutiny, labeling a public trash can as a “ballot box” near a polling site is not a benign error—it is a foreseeable and functional act of voter suppression. The claim’s core is that the placement was intended to trick voters into discarding ballots. The documented swift removal and apology are consistent with caught malfeasance, not innocent mistake.

Your cited quotes of “certainly not intended” are self-serving denials from the responsible party. Their prompt damage control—including checking for trashed ballots—implies they recognized the immediate, predictable harm, which any reasonable actor would foresee. The absence of an official investigation into Philadelphia does not prove innocence; it reflects the difficulty of prosecuting intent, not the absence of it.

My knowledge of Philadelphia’s election dynamics and the sheer improbability of such a conspicuous “coincidence” makes the “mistake” narrative implausible. The act’s functional outcome—disenfranchising voters through confusion—aligns with the claim of intent. Therefore, the verdict should be “supported.”

**Judge:** The verified quotes from the documents explicitly state the signage was unintentional and that an apology was issued, directly contradicting the claim's assertion of malicious intent. The debater without documents argues for functional intent based on recklessness, but without counterevidence, the documented denial of intent outweighs speculative reasoning.

# Claim 23 — Joe Biden wants to ban fracking

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim that "Joe Biden wants to ban fracking" is refuted by the clear, repeated, and explicit statements in the record. Biden has directly denied this ambition on multiple occasions. The record quotes him stating in a 2020 campaign speech: <v_quote>I am not banning fracking. Let me say that again. I am not banning fracking, no matter how many times Donald Trump lies about me.</v_quote> This is not a vague position but a definitive one.

Furthermore, the record clarifies that his policy platform distinguishes between a nationwide ban and stopping new leasing on federal land. It quotes Biden specifying: <v_quote>I will not ban fracking, I said no fracking on federal land</v_quote>. The sources consistently support this, noting his plan focused on <u_quote>stopping all oil drilling or gas drilling on federal lands</u_quote>, not prohibiting the practice on private lands where the vast majority occurs.

His own words from a debate are recorded as: <v_quote>I never said I oppose fracking</v_quote>. The record finds no evidence of any executive or legislative action toward a comprehensive ban. The claim conflates a targeted federal leasing policy with a desire to eliminate fracking entirely, a misrepresentation the record explicitly contradicts.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
Your opponent’s citations capture a candidate’s explicit campaign denials, but they obscure a deeper political reality. Politicians routinely soften stakes during elections. The more credible evidence lies in Biden’s long-term policy trajectory and his own prior statements. During primary debates, he clearly said, “No more subsidies for fossil fuel industry. No more drilling on federal lands. No more drilling including offshore. No ability for the oil industry to continue to drill, period.” This reveals an ideological endpoint, even if moderated for general election tactics.

The distinction between a “ban” and a phase-out via prohibitive regulation is semantic. His executive actions freezing new federal leases—though later challenged in court—demonstrate an operational strategy to restrict supply and growth, aiming to make the industry unviable over time. A true assessment of “wants” must look beyond soundbites to policy direction and party consensus. The claim captures this underlying ambition more accurately than the opponent’s literal reading of electoral damage control.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The record is unequivocal: Biden's stated desire was explicitly not to ban fracking nationwide. His own words, repeated across campaign speeches and debates, constitute direct evidence of his intent. He stated: <v_quote>I am not banning fracking. Let me say that again. I am not banning fracking</v_quote>. This is not a vague political signal but a clear, declarative statement made in an official campaign capacity.

The opponent’s argument hinges on inferring a hidden "ambition" beyond these explicit denials. However, the record shows Biden himself drew a precise line between a full ban and his federal lands policy. He clarified: <v_quote>I will not ban fracking, I said no fracking on federal land</v_quote>. His platform's focus on <u_quote>stopping all oil drilling or gas drilling on federal lands</u_quote> was a targeted proposal, not a desire to eliminate the industry entirely, which accounts for the majority of activity on private lands.

Furthermore, the record notes he viewed natural gas as a needed "transition fuel," directly contradicting the goal of its elimination. The claim misrepresents a specific policy constraint as a universal desire. Based on the documented statements and platform, the verdict of "refuted" is accurate.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The documented denials from the general election campaign are tactical recalibrations, not refutations of a core ambition. During the primary, Biden’s stated vision was unequivocal: “no more drilling” and ending fossil fuel subsidies. That articulation of an endpoint—phasing out fossil fuels, which includes fracking—defines his underlying policy desire.

Executive actions restricting new federal leasing are not a mere “targeted policy”; they are operational tools to constrict the industry’s future and align with the stated goal of eliminating fossil fuel dependence over time. A de facto ban through regulation and halted expansion is functionally identical to a ban. The semantic distinction between an immediate prohibition and a mandated phase-out collapses when the end goal is elimination.

His general election assurances are politically expedient softening, not a reversal of ideology. The core ambition remains consistent with his primary statements and is materially enacted through policy actions designed to make the industry unviable. Therefore, the claim accurately captures his long-term strategic objective, even if his rhetoric varied for electoral pragmatism.

**Judge:** The debater with documents presented verified quotes where Joe Biden explicitly and repeatedly denied any intention to ban fracking, directly contradicting the claim. The opposing argument relied on speculative inferences about long-term policy goals without providing concrete evidence from the record to support the assertion of desire.

# Claim 25 — McDonald’s Azerbaijan's official account was promoting the military taking of Nagorno-Karabakh.

gold `Supported` · InFact `supported` · after debate `supported`

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The verdict is correct because the verified McDonald's Azerbaijan account actively promoted Azerbaijan's military campaign. The account was confirmed as official, with the Record stating it was <v_quote>the verified account for the fast food giant in Azerbaijan, which has 107k Instagram followers</v_quote>. During the 2020 Nagorno-Karabakh conflict, this account shared explicitly supportive content, such as a post featuring a soldier with the caption <v_quote>"We wish our native flag to always fly on the heights of Azerbaijan!"</v_quote>. Another post stated <v_quote>"Every inch of the country is native and indivisible!"</v_quote>. The Record further clarifies that these posts amounted to sharing <v_quote>"support for Azerbaijan forces"</v_quote> in the conflict, which constitutes promotion of the military operation. The fact that McDonald's Corporation later confirmed the franchisee <v_quote>"removed the social media content"</v_quote> underscores that this was not a neutral or misinterpreted message but an action requiring corporate intervention. Therefore, the evidence directly supports the claim that the official account promoted the military taking.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The quoted posts express general patriotic sentiment about Azerbaijan's territorial integrity, not explicit promotion of military operations. Phrases like "Every inch of the country is native and indivisible!" are common nationalist statements that could appear in peacetime. The critical link—demonstrating these posts actively advocated for or glorified the specific military campaign to retake Nagorno-Karabakh—is absent from the provided excerpts. The account’s removal of content reflects corporate risk management in a sensitive geopolitical context, not an admission that the content constituted promotion of a military operation. A franchisee account sharing a flag emoticon or a territorial slogan falls short of the specific advocacy the claim alleges.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The opponent's attempt to reframe these posts as merely patriotic misrepresents the Record's explicit context. The documents specify that the content was shared "during the 2020 escalation of the conflict" and constituted sharing <v_quote>"support for Azerbaijan forces"</v_quote> in that specific war. A post depicting a soldier holding a flag with a caption wishing for it to fly "on the heights of Azerbaijan" is not a generic peacetime slogan; it is a direct visual and textual endorsement of the military outcome in the contested region of Nagorno-Karabakh. The Record also confirms this was original content from the verified official account, not a reshared general statement. Furthermore, the corporate response that the franchisee <v_quote>"removed the social media content"</v_quote> validates that the posts were controversial and directly linked to the military conflict, not that they were benign national pride. The evidence directly supports the verdict.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The opponent conflates general patriotic sentiment with explicit promotion of a military operation. Phrases about territorial integrity are common political discourse, not inherently advocacy for specific military action. The quoted posts lack any direct mention of the Nagorno-Karabakh military operation, glorification of combat, or calls to support it. 

A franchisee’s social media post expressing national unity is not equivalent to the corporate entity “promoting” a military campaign. The opponent’s argument relies entirely on interpreting context, but the claim requires clear promotional intent. Corporate removal of content can reflect sensitivity to geopolitical controversy, not confirmation of the content’s nature. 

The provided excerpts do not demonstrate that the account was actively advocating for or endorsing the military seizure of the region. The verdict overreaches by equating nationalist rhetoric with specific military promotion.

**Judge:** The verified quotes from the documents explicitly state that the account shared 'support for Azerbaijan forces' during the conflict, and the corporate removal of content further underscores the promotional intent. The debater without documents failed to counter this direct evidence from the record.

# Claim 27 — Sleeping under a mosquito bed net treated (or not treated) with insecticide is ineffective and harmful to human health.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict "refuted" is correct because the claim's twin assertions—ineffectiveness and harm—are directly contradicted by the evidence in the record.

First, on effectiveness, the record shows global health authorities and scientific literature endorse bed nets. The WHO recommends insecticide-treated nets as a <v_quote>"cost-effective and efficacious method of controlling malaria."</v_quote> Furthermore, meta-analysis confirms that <v_quote>"both insecticide-treated nets and untreated nets significantly reduce the incidence of malaria compared to no net use."</v_quote> Even untreated nets provide protection, as a clinical trial found <v_quote>"untreated bed nets provide some individual protection against malaria."</v_quote>

Second, on harm, the record finds no evidence for the claim. The CDC states the insecticides used <v_quote>"have been shown to pose very low health risks to humans and other mammals."</v_quote> Crucially, there is no documentation of harm: the record notes <v_quote>no evidence provided for acute poisoning or severe adverse health events from normal use.</v_quote> For untreated nets, no novel health risks are identified.

The record acknowledges factors like insecticide resistance can "undermine the effectiveness," but not that nets become ineffective or harmful overall. Therefore, the claim is comprehensively refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's "refuted" verdict relies on a curated document set that omits critical context. While global health authorities endorse bed nets, that endorsement assumes proper use, sufficient net coverage, and functioning insecticides—conditions often unmet in practice. More importantly, the cited evidence does not negate the claim of harm.

Specifically, pyrethroid insecticides used on nets are associated with documented health concerns. These include skin and respiratory irritation, headaches, and nausea, particularly with prolonged exposure or in poorly ventilated spaces. The CDC's phrase "very low health risks" acknowledges risk, it does not state "no risk." Furthermore, the lack of "evidence provided" in the opponent's documents for severe poisoning does not mean such events never occur; it means their source material didn't include them.

Regarding effectiveness, the rise of insecticide-resistant mosquito populations is a major, well-known crisis that critically undermines net utility. Calling nets broadly "ineffective" is too strong, but declaring the claim comprehensively refuted ignores the real-world challenges of resistance and misuse that compromise both safety and efficacy. The verdict is wrong because it mistakes institutional endorsement for universal, uncontested safety and effectiveness.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent's argument about real-world challenges does not undermine the verdict, because the record explicitly accounts for these factors while still affirming effectiveness and safety. The record acknowledges that insecticide resistance "may undermine the effectiveness of nets," but this is not proof of overall ineffectiveness. Instead, it clarifies that when coverage is high, nets "have been shown to reduce malaria illness and child mortality" and are "associated with sharp decreases in malaria." This shows the record weighs the challenges but still concludes nets are effective.

On harm, the opponent speculates about risks "often unmet in practice," but the record presents the actual scientific assessment. It states the insecticides used "have been shown to pose very low health risks to humans and other mammals" and finds "no evidence provided for acute poisoning or severe adverse health events from normal use." For untreated nets, the record identifies "no novel or increased risk." The claim asserts harm as a fact, but the evidence in the record does not support that assertion.

The verdict is correct because the record's findings directly refute both pillars of the claim.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The verdict "supported" is correct because the claim accurately highlights the real-world limitations that the opponent's documents only partially acknowledge. On effectiveness, the opponent concedes that insecticide resistance "may undermine" net utility. This isn't a minor caveat; it's a well-documented global crisis rendering nets less effective in many regions. Calling nets ineffective broadly may be overstated, but asserting they are universally effective, as the fact-check does, ignores this critical context that the claim correctly identifies.

On harm, the documents' phrase "very low health risks" does not equate to "no risk." The claim of harm is supported by the acknowledged existence of risk itself. Furthermore, "no evidence provided" for acute poisoning is a statement about the document store's contents, not about the absence of such events in reality. Pyrethroid exposure is associated with neurological and respiratory symptoms in medical literature, a context absent from the opponent's citations.

The fact-check mistakes institutional endorsement for unconditional truth, while the claim correctly points out that in practice, nets can be both less effective and carry demonstrable, if low, health risks. The verdict is wrong because it denies the documented complexities.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing that bed nets are effective in reducing malaria and that insecticides pose very low health risks with no evidence of severe harm, directly contradicting the claim's assertions. The debater without documents' arguments about insecticide resistance and low risk do not negate this documented evidence of overall safety and efficacy.

# Claim 28 — Pogba Has Quit The French National Team Over Macron's Remarks on Islam.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict is correct because the claim is directly contradicted by the documented facts. The Record establishes that Pogba never officially quit; he actively denied the reports. He used Instagram to dismiss the story, calling it <v_quote>"Unacceptable. Fake news"</v_quote>. No official confirmation came from his agents or the French Football Federation, and sources consistently used tentative language like <v_quote>"reportedly"</v_quote>.

Furthermore, Pogba's continued involvement with the team disproves the claim. After the reports emerged, he participated in France's friendly match against Finland on November 11, 2020, as the Record confirms <u_quote>France’s next fixture after the controversy was a friendly match against Finland on Nov. 11</u_quote>. This active participation is incompatible with having quit the team. While some reports alleged Macron's remarks were the cause, these were unverified and explicitly denied by the subject himself. The claim of his retirement over the remarks is therefore factually false.

His earlier, vehement denial was a reaction to premature reports, not a permanent retraction. The subsequent, actual retirement is the operative fact that supports the claim. A document store that fails to record this later, confirmed action is incomplete. The claim is not about the 2020 rumors but about the reason behind his ultimate departure, which he publicly stated. Therefore, the verdict should be `supported`.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
