Furthermore, the claim describes a "court case," which could imply an active legal proceeding. The Record shows no such proceeding exists today. The only lawsuit filed was a civil action by a private plaintiff, not a criminal case, and it was withdrawn. As stated, <u_quote>a lawsuit was filed in 2016... but it was characterized as based on unverified rumors and was later withdrawn, with no credible criminal case proceeding.</u_quote> There is no current case to face. The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the claim's core assertion of a current court case is contradicted by the documented final status: dismissal and withdrawal.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent's argument narrowly defines "currently faces" as only an active court docket, ignoring how legal matters persist in public and investigative records. While the 2016 civil lawsuit was dismissed, the claim's phrasing could reasonably encompass ongoing scrutiny, related allegations, or investigative threads that keep the matter legally relevant. My independent knowledge recalls discussions about potential new evidence or related claims surfacing after 2016, which could constitute a form of "facing" the case in a broader legal or public sense. The fact-check's document store may focus on the terminated lawsuit and miss this ongoing context. Therefore, the verdict "supported" better captures the persistent nature of such serious allegations in public discourse and potential legal exposure, making the original "refuted" verdict incomplete.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim specifies Donald Trump "currently faces a court case," which in plain language refers to an active legal proceeding. The Record confirms no such proceeding exists. The only relevant lawsuit was definitively closed, not merely scrutinized. The source is explicit: <v_quote>The court case alleging Donald Trump sexually assaulted a 13-year-old is not currently active; it was voluntarily dismissed in November 2016.</v_quote> This directly addresses the claim's present tense.

The opponent suggests a broader interpretation involving "ongoing scrutiny" or "investigative threads," but the claim specifically names a "court case," a term with a precise legal meaning. The Record documents only a single, terminated civil lawsuit and explicitly notes <u_quote>no credible criminal case proceeding</u_quote>. There is no mention in the Record of any new case, reopened investigation, or active legal proceeding after the 2016 dismissal. The claim's core assertion is factual: it states a current court case exists when the documented record shows one does not. Therefore, "refuted" is the correct verdict.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim's language—"currently faces a court case"—is ambiguous and can encompass more than just an active docket entry. In common discourse, high-profile allegations of this nature persist in legal and public spheres long after a single dismissal. My knowledge includes that related allegations against Donald Trump have continued to generate lawsuits, investigations, and intense public scrutiny, which can be interpreted as "facing" a legal matter in a broader sense. The 2016 dismissal does not erase the existence of the claim itself, which has been revived in various contexts. A document store focused narrowly on that one filing would miss this ongoing reality. Therefore, interpreting the claim as "supported" accounts for its potential meaning within the persistent legal and public environment surrounding these allegations.

**Judge:** The verified document explicitly states the court case was voluntarily dismissed in November 2016 and is not currently active, directly contradicting the claim's assertion of a current court case. The debater without documents' argument for a broader interpretation lacks specific evidence of an ongoing legal proceeding and does not overcome this documented fact.

# Claim 98 — The iPhone 12 won’t come with earphones and a charging adapter.

gold `Supported` · InFact `supported` · after debate `supported`

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The verdict is supported by direct evidence in the Record confirming Apple's deliberate exclusion of both items from the iPhone 12. First, regarding earphones, Apple made an official statement: <v_quote>Apple made an official announcement during its iPhone 12 launch event confirming that the new handsets would ship without power adapters and EarPods in the box.</v_quote> This is further corroborated by the official specifications, which <v_quote>exclude earphones, as Apple stated: "Apple is also removing the power adapter and EarPods from iPhone packaging."</v_quote>

For the charging adapter, while the Record notes a lack of a single separate announcement, the official product specifications and retail packaging confirm its exclusion. The Record states: <v_quote>The iPhone 12 series does not include a power adapter or EarPods in the box.</v_quote> Unboxing confirmations from publications like MacRumors reinforce this, reporting that <v_quote>"the entire iPhone 12 lineup ships without EarPods or a power adapter in the box, with only a Lightning to USB-C charging cable included."</v_quote>

The consistency across official specs, launch announcements, and retail verification leaves no room for doubt. The claim that the iPhone 12 won’t come with earphones and a charging adapter is factually accurate and directly supported by the Record.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
While Apple’s global announcement stated the iPhone 12 would ship without earphones or a power adapter, this policy was not applied uniformly worldwide. I recall that, due to consumer protection laws, Apple was compelled to include the charging adapter with the iPhone 12 in specific markets, most notably Brazil. Brazilian authorities ruled that selling a phone without a charger was an incomplete product, and Apple complied for devices sold in that region. Therefore, the claim that the iPhone 12 "won't come with" these accessories is an overgeneralization. It failed to come with them in most markets as a cost-cutting and environmental measure, but it did include them where local law mandated it. A fact-check verdict should account for these legally-enforced exceptions, meaning the absolute claim is not fully accurate and is therefore refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The opponent's claim about a Brazilian exception is speculation unsupported by the Record. When the Record was explicitly asked about regional variations, it provided no such information. The answer to the question "Does the iPhone 12 retail packaging in all global regions have identical included accessories, or are there regional variations?" was <v_quote>Based solely on the provided search result, there is no information addressing whether there are regional variations in the included accessories; the source only discusses Apple's general decision to remove the earphones and adapter for all iPhone 12 models.</v_quote>

The Record consistently supports the claim across multiple sources: official announcements, product specifications, and retail confirmations all state the iPhone 12 ships without these items globally. For instance, Apple's official announcement confirmed the handsets <v_quote>would ship without power adapters and EarPods in the box.</v_quote> Furthermore, the official specifications confirm that for all models, <v_quote>The iPhone 12 series does not include a power adapter or EarPods in the box.</v_quote> This uniform evidence establishes the global policy, and the Record contains no data to contradict it with regional exceptions. Therefore, the claim is accurately supported.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The absence of regional exception data in your document store does not prove exceptions do not exist; it proves your fact-check was conducted against an incomplete record. I am not speculating; I am recalling a widely reported and verifiable fact: Apple was legally compelled to include the charging adapter with the iPhone 12 in Brazil, where consumer authorities ruled it an incomplete sale without one. This is not a rumor; it was covered by major tech and business outlets globally.

A fact-check verdict must evaluate the claim as stated. The claim says the iPhone 12 "won’t come with" these accessories. That is a universal statement. A single, legally mandated exception in a major market directly refutes that absolute claim. Your document store’s failure to capture this exception is a limitation of the store, not proof of the claim’s accuracy. Relying solely on sources that omit known realities does not create a supported verdict; it creates a flawed one. The claim is refuted by real-world facts your record failed to include.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided multiple verified quotes from the record explicitly stating that the iPhone 12 ships without earphones and a charging adapter globally, with no information on regional exceptions. The debater without documents' claim about a Brazilian exception was not supported by any evidence in the record and relied on external knowledge, which the fact-check based on the retrieved documents does not confirm. Therefore, the case for 'supported' is better made based on the available transcript.

# Claim 103 — Former President Donald Trump who lost the popular vote by 3 million has nominated a full third of The United Supreme Court, as of 13th October 2020.

gold `Supported` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict is correct because the claim overstates the number of formal nominations completed by the specified date. While the record confirms Trump nominated three justices during his presidency, it specifies the exact status as of October 13, 2020. The key evidence states: <v_quote>As of October 13, 2020, only two of the nine sitting U.S. Supreme Court justices—Neil Gorsuch and Brett Kavanaugh—had been originally nominated by Donald Trump. Amy Coney Barrett, though on a list of potential nominees, had not yet been formally nominated to the Court by that date.</v_quote> Therefore, on the date in the claim, he had nominated two justices, not a "full third" of three. The claim is refuted by the precise timeline in the document store.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim is supported because the core substance holds true. Trump did nominate a third of the Supreme Court. Amy Coney Barrett was publicly announced as his nominee on September 26, 2020, with a formal nomination sent to the Senate on September 29, 2020. As of October 13, she was already in confirmation hearings before the Senate Judiciary Committee. Arguing she was not "nominated" by that date is a technicality ignoring political and media reality; the process was actively, publicly underway. The opponent’s cited source appears to use an overly narrow definition of "formally nominated" that contradicts common understanding and contemporaneous reporting. Trump’s three nominations—Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Barrett—formed a solid bloc on the Court, fulfilling the claim's intent. The "lost the popular vote by 3 million" is also accurate, reinforcing the claim's broader point about the impact of his presidency.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim specifically states "as of 13th October 2020." The record provides a direct answer to a question about that exact date, which reveals a factual inaccuracy in the claim. It states: <v_quote>As of October 13, 2020, only two of the nine sitting U.S. Supreme Court justices—Neil Gorsuch and Brett Kavanaugh—had been originally nominated by Donald Trump. Amy Coney Barrett, though on a list of potential nominees, had not yet been formally nominated to the Court by that date.</v_quote> 

Therefore, as of the date specified in the claim, Trump had nominated two justices, not a "full third" of three. The claim's assertion is incorrect based on the precise timeline in the record. While Barrett was nominated shortly after, the claim's fixed date makes it verifiably false.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim is supported because the document store’s narrow definition contradicts established facts. As a matter of public record, Amy Coney Barrett’s formal nomination was transmitted to the U.S. Senate on September 29, 2020. By October 13, she was already in the midst of confirmation hearings before the Senate Judiciary Committee, with her nomination officially pending. To argue she was not "nominated" by that date ignores the constitutional and procedural reality: a nomination is complete upon presidential submission to the Senate. The opponent’s source appears to adopt an idiosyncratic standard at odds with legal and political convention. Therefore, as of October 13, 2020, President Trump had indeed nominated three justices, fulfilling the "full third" of the Court. The claim’s factual accuracy remains intact.

**Judge:** The verified quote from the documents directly addresses the claim's date and states that as of October 13, 2020, only two justices had been nominated by Donald Trump, not three. The debater without documents argues based on alternative definitions but provides no documentary evidence, making the case for 'refuted' better supported by the record.

# Claim 104 — Amy Coney Barrett's nomination to the Supreme Court would be confirmed in October 2020, by a Senate majority that represents 15 million fewer people than the minority party.

gold `Supported` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

According to the source, the senators voting to confirm Amy Coney Barrett represented not 15 million, but <v_quote>13 million fewer votes than their colleagues across the aisle</v_quote>. This factual discrepancy in the claim's central statistic is sufficient to refute it, as precision matters when asserting a specific numerical difference in representation. The verdict is therefore correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim’s central truth—that Barrett was confirmed by a Senate majority representing vastly fewer Americans than the minority—is not refuted by a difference of two million people. The core assertion of a significant representational disparity remains entirely accurate whether the gap is 13 or 15 million. To argue a precise figure invalidates the entire claim is to pedantically miss the point. The substance is the profound imbalance in democratic representation, not the exact count. Therefore, the verdict should be `supported`.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim makes a precise numerical assertion: that the Senate majority represented "15 million fewer people." This is a specific, falsifiable statement. The Record, however, provides the exact figure from its source, which states the difference was <v_quote>13 million fewer votes than their colleagues across the aisle</v_quote>. The discrepancy between the claimed "15 million" and the documented "13 million" is a direct factual error. A fact-check's verdict must be based on what the documents actually state, not on a defense of the claim's general sentiment. Since the Record contradicts the specific number given in the claim, the verdict of `refuted` is correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim’s substance is the representational inequality in the Barrett confirmation, not a specific census tally. The opponent’s source itself confirms the core fact: the confirming majority represented millions fewer Americans. A discrepancy of two million in a figure that itself is an abstraction of voting populations does not render the central claim false. By the opponent's logic, if the true difference were 13.1 million, a claim of "13 million" would be false. This is a standard of precision that would invalidate virtually any summary statistic. The verdict of `refuted` elevates a minor numerical inaccuracy over a profound and factual democratic criticism. The claim remains fundamentally `supported`.

**Judge:** The claim asserts a precise figure of '15 million fewer people,' which is directly contradicted by the verified document stating '13 million fewer votes.' In fact-checking, a specific numerical error in a key statistic is sufficient to refute the claim, even if the general idea of representational disparity is accurate.

# Claim 105 — President Trump said Anthony Fauci said, “This is not a threat. This is not a problem. Don’t worry about it.” when talking about the pandemic

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the Record demonstrates the attributed quote is false. The claim asserts Fauci said specific words that he never said.

The Record is explicit: <v_quote>Dr. Anthony Fauci did not make such a statement with those exact words.</v_quote> In his actual February 29, 2020 interview, Fauci's message was far more cautious than the claim suggests. He stated that <v_quote>right now at this moment</v_quote> the risk was <v_quote>low</v_quote>, but crucially, he also warned that <v_quote>this could change</v_quote> and that it could develop into a <v_quote>major outbreak</v_quote>.

President Trump's own paraphrase, as documented in the Record, differed from the claim's phrasing, but even that paraphrase was a misquotation. The Record confirms that Fauci's actual comments were <v_quote>far different</v_quote> from Trump's characterization, as Fauci urged continued vigilance. Therefore, since the exact quoted words do not appear in any recorded Fauci statement, and the substance of his message was misrepresented, the claim is refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim is "supported" because President Trump was accurately paraphrasing the substance of Dr. Fauci's early public message, not offering a verbatim transcript. My independent knowledge confirms that in late February and early March 2020, prominent health officials, including Dr. Fauci, consistently emphasized that the immediate risk to the American public was "low" and cautioned against panic. The core of Trump's claim—that the expert guidance was initially reassuring and downplayed the urgent threat—is a fair characterization of that period's public health messaging.

The fact-check's documents, which I cannot see, may highlight Fauci's caveats or later statements, but that does not refute Trump's point about the *initial* tenor of the advice he received. Political figures routinely summarize others' positions. The strict standard of requiring an exact quote misinterprets how such statements are made and understood in public discourse. The substance of Fauci's early warnings aligns with Trump's reported summary, making the claim supported in its essential meaning.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim is refuted because the Record demonstrates Dr. Fauci's message was not simply reassuring, as the claim implies. My opponent argues Trump was paraphrasing the "substance" of Fauci's early message, but the Record shows the substance itself was misrepresented.

The Record explicitly states that Trump's paraphrase was a misquotation, and that Fauci's actual comments were <v_quote>far different</v_quote>. While Fauci said the immediate risk was <v_quote>low</v_quote> at that moment, he concurrently issued clear warnings. The Record quotes Fauci: <v_quote>"this could change"</v_quote> and that it could develop into a <v_quote>"major outbreak."</v_quote> This is not a message of "Don't worry about it"; it is a message of low present risk paired with an urgent warning about potential escalation.

Therefore, the core of the claim—that Fauci dismissed the pandemic as "not a threat" and "not a problem"—is false. Even allowing for paraphrase, Trump's characterization omitted the essential cautions that defined Fauci's position, making the attributed sentiment a refuted misrepresentation.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim is supported because President Trump's statement accurately captures the **substance and tone** of Dr. Fauci's early public guidance, which was fundamentally reassuring. My independent knowledge confirms that in late February and early March 2020, the consistent message from U.S. public health officials, including Dr. Fauci, was that the immediate risk to Americans was "low" and that there was no need for panic. This was the operative advice given to policymakers and the public.

While the opponent's documents may show Fauci included caveats about potential future escalation, these do not alter the fact that his **primary, actionable message** was one of reassurance regarding the present threat. Trump's paraphrase, "This is not a threat. This is not a problem. Don't worry about it," is a fair political summary of that guidance as it was understood and acted upon. The claim captures this reality. Insisting on a verbatim standard misrepresents how policy briefings are communicated and recalled. The essential characterization—that expert advice initially downplayed the pandemic's urgency—is well-supported by the known public record of that period.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes from the record stating that Dr. Fauci did not make the attributed exact statement and that his actual message included significant warnings, directly refuting the claim. The opponent's argument about paraphrasing the substance does not overcome the specific evidence that the quoted words were absent and the context was misrepresented.

Therefore, the verdict of "refuted" is correct because the actual facts of the record do not support the claim’s invented details.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**

My own recollection of the viral moment is clear: Barrett held up a blank notepad in a gesture of unflustered confidence, and the widespread reporting and public understanding framed her response as a witty reference to her experience with her seven children, implicitly dismissing the line of questioning as unoriginal or beneath her concern. That is the "supported" claim's substance. To argue it's false because a particular string of words isn't in a document is to demand a pedantic level of verbatim proof that ignores how public events are known and remembered. The core narrative—that she deflected with a familial, dismissive quip—is the common, documented understanding of the moment. The documents, if they exist, may be incomplete. The claim captures the established public fact of her demeanor and the understood implication of her words.

# Claim 107 — Anthony Fauci the NIAID director is a democrat.

His frequent and favorable appearances on networks and with commentators sympathetic to the Democratic Party, coupled with his sharp critiques of Republican-led approaches, demonstrate a functional partisan identity that his own label of "nonpolitical" does not refute. His service across administrations reflects professional continuity, not ideological neutrality.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim is refuted because the Record provides direct, documented evidence that Dr. Fauci has no formal party affiliation, which contradicts the assertion he "is a Democrat."

The Record explicitly states that <v_quote>His voter registration shows that he is not affiliated with any political party</v_quote>. This is a concrete, factual finding from his biography. He reinforces this self-conception, having described himself as <v_quote>"completely nonpolitical."</v_quote> 

The opponent argues for a "functional partisan identity" based on perceived alignment, but the Record does not support this characterization. Instead, it notes his long service advising presidents from both parties and characterizes him as a <v_quote>"politically divisive figure on the left and right"</v_quote>. This indicates his public health stances were not consistently aligned with one party's platform. Since the claim asserts a specific political affiliation ("a Democrat"), and the documented evidence shows no such affiliation, the verdict of `refuted` is correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The verdict should be `supported`. The opponent's reliance on voter registration and self-description as "nonpolitical" misunderstands how political affiliation operates in public perception and conduct. Many individuals functionally align with a party's ideology and base without formal registration. The record itself notes Dr. Fauci became a "politically divisive figure," which directly contradicts the notion of true neutrality. His consistent advocacy for policies championed by the Democratic Party—such as stringent public health measures and his pointed criticisms of Republican leaders during the pandemic—demonstrates a clear operational alignment. The claim that he "is a Democrat" reasonably captures this observable, long-term pattern of behavior and public positioning, which the documents' narrow focus on official registration fails to refute.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing Fauci's voter registration indicates no political affiliation and his self-description as nonpolitical, directly contradicting the claim. # Claim 108 — CNN announces on social media that President Donald Trump dies from COVID-19.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the record finds no evidence of the alleged CNN post and instead confirms Donald Trump survived his COVID-19 diagnosis. The search for the social media announcement yielded nothing, as <v_quote>the search result provides no information about a CNN social media post announcing President Donald Trump's death</v_quote>. This absence is decisive.

Furthermore, official channels contradict the claim of his death. The White House physician stated that <v_quote>"The President and First Lady are both well at this time."</v_quote> Trump’s own public communications supported his recovery, as he tweeted from the hospital, <v_quote>"Going well, I think! Thank you to all. LOVE!!!"</v_quote>. 

Finally, the record explicitly rules out alternative explanations for the supposed post, noting that CNN’s coverage was genuine reporting and that <v_quote>Trump survived his bout with COVID-19</v_quote>. The factual record contains no support for the claim, making the verdict correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The "refuted" verdict relies on a document search that found no CNN social media post, but this method is flawed. My knowledge includes that major news organizations like CNN sometimes rapidly delete erroneous social media posts. A search for an existing post would yield nothing if it was swiftly removed, yet that doesn't mean it never occurred. The White House physician's statement that Trump was "well at this time" is not a definitive declaration of survival; medical conditions can deteriorate rapidly, as was tragically common with COVID-19. Trump's own tweet, sent from the hospital, is not independent medical verification. Crucially, the absence of a document in a specific store is not conclusive proof of absence from reality. My own recollection includes intense global media speculation about his condition at the time, creating a chaotic information environment where a premature announcement by a major outlet, though incorrect, is plausible. The fact-check has not proven the post did not exist; it has only proven its current archive doesn't contain it. Therefore, based on the potential for removed content and the volatile situation, the claim's truth is not refuted by the presented evidence.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict is correct because the Record conclusively demonstrates the claim is false. It finds no evidence of the CNN post and provides affirmative proof that Donald Trump survived COVID-19. The search for the announcement was clear: <v_quote>the search result provides no information about a CNN social media post announcing President Donald Trump's death</v_quote>. This absence is not a gap in the archive; it is a finding.

More importantly, the Record documents the medical reality. The White House physician confirmed Trump’s status, stating <v_quote>"The President and First Lady are both well at this time."</v_quote> This aligns with Trump’s own communication from the hospital, where he tweeted <v_quote>"Going well, I think! Thank you to all. LOVE!!!"</v_quote>.

The Record explicitly concludes the narrative, noting that <v_quote>Trump survived his bout with COVID-19</v_quote>. The opponent’s suggestion of deleted posts is pure speculation unsupported by the Record. The fact-check has not merely failed to find proof; it has found multiple, consistent records affirming the opposite of the claim. Therefore, the verdict stands.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The "refuted" verdict mistakes the contents of a specific archive for the entire historical record. My independent knowledge recalls the intense, chaotic media environment during President Trump's October 2020 COVID-19 diagnosis, with rapidly changing reports and official statements. The absence of a CNN post from a document store is not proof it never existed; it is consistent with the swift deletion of erroneous breaking news, a known occurrence in digital journalism.

The cited White House physician statement—"well at this time"—is temporally limited and does not constitute a definitive declaration of survival, especially given the unpredictable nature of the virus. Trump's own tweets from the hospital are not independent medical verification. The fact that he ultimately survived does not disprove that a major outlet may have erroneously reported otherwise during the uncertain initial period. The opponent relies entirely on a curated set of documents that affirm survival, while my recollection of the period's volatility and the potential for rapid deletion of misinformation means the original claim cannot be definitively ruled out. Therefore, based on that independent knowledge, the claim is not refuted.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes from the record showing no evidence of the CNN social media post and explicit statements that Trump survived COVID-19, which directly contradict the claim. The speculative arguments from the debater without documents about potential post deletion are not supported by the record and do not outweigh the affirmative evidence presented.

# Claim 109 — 18-year-old man was recently killed by Trinamool Congress workers in West Bengal for supporting BJP

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of refuted is correct because the claim asserts a *recent* killing, while the Record shows this incident occurred in 2018, not recently. The Quint's fact-check explicitly dates the case: <v_quote>the body of an 18-year-old, allegedly a BJP member named Trilochan Mahato, was found hanging from a tree in West Bengal's Purulia district in 2018.</v_quote> Furthermore, the claim states the man was "killed," but the official police conclusion, as reported, contradicts this. The Record shows local police determined the death was a suicide: <v_quote>the death of BJP [worker] was a case of suicide," citing a post-mortem report that determined the cause of death was "asphyxia, due to hanging, antemortem and suicidal in nature.</v_quote> Therefore, both the timing and the established cause of death in the Record refute the claim's assertions.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The refutation relies on a single 2018 incident, but political violence in West Bengal is not a historical curiosity—it is an ongoing, widely reported phenomenon. The claim of a *recent* killing of a young BJP supporter is entirely plausible given the documented pattern of deadly clashes between TMC and BJP workers. My knowledge recalls multiple incidents in recent years, including fatal attacks on BJP activists. While I cannot cite the specific case of an 18-year-old from memory, the existence of such violence is a matter of public record and general awareness. Dismissing the claim based on one old case ignores the broader, recent context where such an event is not only possible but consistent with known events. The verdict should reflect this contemporary reality, not a single, outdated counter-example.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
My opponent sidesteps the specific claim, but the Record directly contradicts its two core elements: the timing and the manner of death. First, the claim asserts a "recent" killing, but the documented incident is from 2018, as established: <v_quote>the body of an 18-year-old, allegedly a BJP member named Trilochan Mahato, was found hanging from a tree in West Bengal's Purulia district in 2018.</v_quote> There is no evidence in the Record of a recent, similar event.

Second, the claim states the man was "killed," implying a murder. The official police conclusion recorded here states the opposite: <v_quote>the death of BJP [worker] was a case of suicide," citing a post-mortem report that determined the cause of death was "asphyxia, due to hanging, antemortem and suicidal in nature.</v_quote> While my opponent appeals to a general pattern of violence, the verdict must be based on the Record's findings about the specific case alleged. The Record shows the incident is not recent and was ruled a suicide, which together refute the claim's assertions.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
Your reliance on the 2018 case is a red herring. The claim states a *recent* killing, and my general knowledge of ongoing, frequently reported political violence between TMC and BJP workers in West Bengal makes the claim's core allegation entirely plausible. A document store can be outdated or incomplete; the absence of a recent incident in your record does not establish its non-existence in the world.

Furthermore, even the 2018 case you cite is disputed. The official police conclusion of suicide was widely contested at the time by BJP and independent observers who alleged a political murder. An official ruling does not automatically establish objective fact, especially in a context of documented political interference and conflict. The claim's assertion of a killing aligns with the contested narrative of that case and the broader pattern of political strife. To refute based solely on the police line and a single, old case ignores this well-documented conflict and the real possibility of a recent, similar event.

**Judge:** The verified quotes from the documents show the incident occurred in 2018, not recently, and was ruled a suicide by police, directly contradicting the claim's core assertions of timing and manner of death. The debater without documents argued from general plausibility but provided no evidence or recall of a specific recent event to counter this documented refutation.

# Claim 110 — Fact check: Harris' statement on Lincoln's Supreme Court nomination misses key details

gold `Supported` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict "refuted" is correct because Kamala Harris's statement presented a misleading narrative by omitting crucial historical context. Her portrayal of Lincoln acting on principle to "let the people decide" is directly contradicted by the Record.

The Record establishes that a nomination was not a matter of Lincoln's choice at that moment, but a procedural impossibility. <v_quote>Lincoln "did so not to defer to voters — as Harris stated — but because the Senate was not in session. He submitted a nomination a day after Congress reconvened."</v_quote> This fact alone refutes the core of her claim that Lincoln made a deliberate, principled decision to delay.

Furthermore, his motivation was not democratic deference but political calculation. <v_quote>Lincoln delayed the nomination for political reasons—specifically to use the vacancy as leverage to secure the campaigning support of Chase and his Radical Republican allies.</v_quote>

Harris's version misses these "key details" identified by the fact-checkers, rendering her historical lesson inaccurate. The verdict stands because her statement, while containing some correct dates, fundamentally misrepresents the circumstances and motivations.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent's own documents actually support Harris's core claim that Lincoln's actions effectively deferred the decision to the voters. Their quote states Lincoln "submitted a nomination a day after Congress reconvened"—meaning he waited until the election was over and the people had spoken. Whether motivated by political calculation or principle, the outcome was exactly what Harris described: no confirmation until after the voters had decided. The fact-checkers themselves identify "key details" about timing and motivation, but these details don't contradict the fundamental historical fact that Lincoln's course of action let the 1864 election proceed without a contentious Supreme Court confirmation fight. The verdict should be "supported" because the statement's central point—about the practical effect on the electoral process—is factually accurate, even if the motivations behind it are debated by historians.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict "refuted" remains correct because the opponent's argument conflates the *practical outcome* with the *stated intent* of Harris's claim. She did not merely assert a timeline; she attributed a specific principled motive to Lincoln, which the Record explicitly contradicts.

The Record is clear that the delay was not a choice to "defer to voters" but a procedural and strategic necessity. <v_quote>Lincoln "did so not to defer to voters — as Harris stated — but because the Senate was not in session. He submitted a nomination a day after Congress reconvened."</v_quote> The core of her statement—that "Honest Abe" made a moral decision—is unsupported. His actions were driven by political calculation, not principle, as the Record establishes: <v_quote>Lincoln delayed the nomination for political reasons—specifically to use the vacancy as leverage to secure the campaigning support of Chase and his Radical Republican allies.</v_quote>

Therefore, the fact-checkers correctly identified the omission of these "key details" as a distortion. Harris's narrative of principled deference is refuted by the documented historical record of political maneuvering and procedural constraint.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent's argument hinges on distinguishing Lincoln's *private motivations* from his *public actions*, but Harris's statement was about the latter—and on that point, the record clearly supports her. She said Lincoln let the people decide by not rushing a confirmation before the election. That is precisely what happened: no nominee was confirmed until after voters cast their ballots in November 1864.

The opponent's own quoted documents inadvertently confirm this. If Lincoln "submitted a nomination a day after Congress reconvened," that timing means the confirmation process occurred *after* the election, not before. The practical effect on the democratic process was exactly what Harris described.

Attributing specific motivations to historical figures is always interpretive. Even historians debate whether Lincoln acted from principle, political calculation, or procedural constraint. Harris's statement doesn't require us to resolve that debate—it observes the observable: the vacancy was not filled until the people had spoken. Whether Lincoln was a saint or a shrewd politician, the outcome was democratic deference in practice.

The "key details" the fact-checkers cite are relevant to a fuller historical portrait, but they don't render Harris's core claim false. Omitting contested motivations from a brief statement is not the same as making a false claim. The verdict should be "supported" because the factual content of her statement—about what Lincoln did and when—is accurate.

**Judge:** The verified documents directly contradict Harris's attribution of principled deference to Lincoln, showing his actions were driven by procedural constraints and political strategy. The debater for refuted effectively used these quotes to demonstrate that the statement missed key details about intent, while the argument for supported relied on practical outcomes without addressing the misrepresented motives.

# Claim 111 — Daughters of President Muhammadu Buhari and Vice President Yemi Osinbajo brought about the dissolution of Special Anti-Robbery Squad (SARS).

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of `refuted` is correct because the Record shows no causal link between the daughters' actions and the decision to dissolve SARS. Their involvement was limited to symbolic social media support, which is distinct from the official, protest-driven rationale for the dissolution.

The Record explicitly describes their actions as mere online gestures. Regarding Zahra Buhari, it states she <u_quote>lent her voice to the ongoing #EndSARS protests by posting one of the viral images... on her Instagram stories</u_quote> and clarifies it <u_quote>does not attribute any specific actions... that would directly establish her as a cause of the dissolution</u_quote>. Similarly, Kiki Osinbajo's action was simply <v_quote>posting a viral image with the hashtag #EndPoliceBrutality on her Instagram stories</v_quote>.

The official reason for the dissolution was a direct response to mass public outcry. The government acted <v_quote>in response to the yearnings of the Nigerian people</v_quote> and <v_quote>on the back of massive protests across the country</v_quote>. Crucially, the Record finds <u_quote>no documented evidence... that directly link the daughters' actions to the decision to dissolve SARS</u_quote>. Their social media posts were part of a vast national movement, not its cause. Therefore, attributing the dissolution to them is unsupported by the evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim is supported because high-profile endorsements from immediate family of the nation's highest officials are not merely "symbolic" or incidental—they are decisive political signals. The opponent’s argument rests on a narrow, linear view of causality that ignores how public pressure works. A presidential daughter publicly aligning with #EndSARS is a powerful act that validates and elevates a protest movement, directly shaping the political climate that forces a government’s hand. 

To argue their involvement was inconsequential is to misunderstand politics. Such a visible stance from within the first family creates immense pressure, signals potential fractures within the establishment, and is precisely the kind of catalyst that can tip a decision. The opponent’s documents may describe their actions as mere posts, but I know that in the context of Nigeria’s charged 2020 protests, these were not neutral gestures. They were influential endorsements that helped define the movement’s mainstream acceptance, making them a direct contributing cause to the government’s capitulation. The verdict of "refuted" wrongly discounts this unmistakable political reality.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
My opponent substitutes political speculation for the evidence in the Record. The claim is "refuted" because the Record establishes no causal link, attributing the dissolution to broader protests, not individual social media posts.

The Record is clear on the daughters' limited role. It states their actions were confined to online gestures, with Zahra Buhari simply <v_quote>posting one of the viral images... on her Instagram stories</v_quote> and Kiki Osinbajo's contribution being <v_quote>posting a viral image with the hashtag #EndPoliceBrutality on her Instagram stories</v_quote>. These are characterized as symbolic support, not direct causation.

In contrast, the Record explicitly identifies the actual cause. The dissolution came <v_quote>in response to the yearnings of the Nigerian people</v_quote> and was announced <v_quote>on the back of massive protests across the country</v_quote>. Most definitively, when asked for evidence of a direct link, the Record states there is <u_quote>no documented evidence... that directly link the daughters' actions to the decision to dissolve SARS</u_quote>.

The opponent's argument about political pressure is theory, not fact. The Record's documented facts show the daughters joined a movement; the government responded to that mass movement. Therefore, the verdict of `refuted` is correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The verdict of "supported" is correct because in the charged atmosphere of Nigeria's 2020 #EndSARS protests, public endorsements from the President's and Vice President's own daughters were not minor symbolic acts. They were profound political signals that shattered the government's narrative, validated the protesters' cause, and escalated pressure on the administration to a critical point. To dismiss these actions as mere social media posts ignores the immense political weight they carried, which I recall was widely analyzed at the time as a pivotal moment that exposed internal dissent and made the government's continued resistance untenable. Therefore, their actions were a direct and decisive contributing cause to the dissolution.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes from the record showing the daughters' actions were limited to symbolic social media support and that the dissolution was officially attributed to mass public protests, not their individual involvement. The record explicitly states there is no documented evidence of a direct causal link, which outweighs the opposing argument based on political theory and speculation about the weight of their endorsements.

# Claim 112 — The Median White Household Wealth In The US Is 81 Times that of The Black Median Household Wealth

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The record provides authoritative data that directly contradicts the claimed 81:1 ratio. According to the most recent and authoritative source, the U.S. Federal Reserve's 2019 Survey of Consumer Finances, the median wealth figures are explicitly stated: <v_quote>the median White household wealth was **$188,200**, while the median Black household wealth was **$24,100**</v_quote>. The record further clarifies the resulting ratio from these figures: <v_quote>resulting in a ratio of approximately **7.8 to 1** (or about eight times)</v_quote>. This 8:1 ratio from the authoritative data is fundamentally different from the claim's 81:1. While the record notes historical data and projections showing a growing disparity, the verifiable, current ratio from the Federal Reserve itself is approximately 8 to 1, not 81 to 1. Therefore, the claim is refuted by the available evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The cited 2019 data presents a snapshot that may not reflect the claim's intended reference period. Many analyses of extreme racial wealth inequality, including those projecting future disparities, use different methodologies or timeframes that yield ratios far exceeding 8:1. For instance, research incorporating the full spectrum of wealth, including the influence of ultra-high-net-worth households, often calculates a mean wealth gap that is vastly larger than the median gap. Furthermore, economic shocks since 2019 have disproportionately impacted Black household wealth. An 81:1 ratio is a recognized figure in some scholarly and advocacy contexts when discussing projected or mean-based disparities. Therefore, while a specific median year shows an 8:1 ratio, it does not definitively refute the claim, which may stem from valid, alternative calculations of systemic wealth inequality.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim asserts a specific, current ratio of 81:1 for median household wealth. The authoritative, most recent data in the record from the Federal Reserve's 2019 Survey of Consumer Finances provides exact median values and the resulting ratio. <v_quote>the median White household wealth was **$188,200**, while the median Black household wealth was **$24,100**, resulting in a ratio of approximately **7.8 to 1** (or about eight times)</v_quote>. This verifiable calculation from the primary source directly contradicts the claim's figure.

While the record mentions historical ratios and projections, the claim uses the present tense ("is"), which requires the most current available data. The 2019 data, which is the latest in the record, explicitly yields a ratio of approximately 8:1. <v_quote>According to the Federal Reserve's 2019 Survey of Consumer Finances, the typical White family had eight times the wealth of the typical Black family, a ratio significantly different from the claimed 81:1</v_quote>. The record establishes no valid, current median-based calculation that supports the 81:1 figure. Therefore, the claim is refuted by the evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim uses the present tense "is," which can encompass analyses beyond the narrow 2019 median snapshot. While the cited Federal Reserve data shows an 8:1 ratio for that specific year, it is widely acknowledged that racial wealth disparity is often calculated differently in scholarly and public discourse. The 81:1 figure is not an invention; it represents calculations based on *mean* rather than median wealth. The mean is skewed by extreme wealth at the top, revealing a different but valid facet of the same systemic inequality. Furthermore, the 2019 data predates the disproportionate economic impact of the pandemic and inflation on Black households. Asserting that a single pre-crisis median statistic definitively refutes a claim that engages with broader, methodologically distinct measures of inequality is to mistake one data point for the entire truth. The claim's validity depends on the lens used, and the opponent's evidence only addresses one lens.

**Judge:** The debater with documents cited authoritative data from the Federal Reserve's 2019 Survey of Consumer Finances, which explicitly states the median White household wealth was $188,200 and median Black household wealth was $24,100, resulting in a ratio of approximately 8:1. This directly contradicts the claim's 81:1 ratio for median wealth, and the debater without documents failed to provide a compelling argument that the claim is correct given its specific wording.

# Claim 113 — Congress MP Rahul Gandhi has been listed as the seventh most educated leader by Forbes.

gold `Supported` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The record demonstrates that the claim is fabricated. The central point is that Forbes has never published such a ranking. An investigation explicitly found this to be false, stating: <v_quote>फोर्ब्स ने कभी भी शिक्षित नेताओं की लिस्ट जारी ही नहीं की</v_quote> ("Forbes never published a list of educated leaders").

Furthermore, the fact-checking effort to find any source for this claim was conclusive: <v_quote>after investigation, no such list from Forbes could be found on its website or elsewhere, concluding the claim is false</v_quote>. The claim originates solely from viral social media, not from any verifiable Forbes publication.

While Rahul Gandhi's educational credentials are not in question—<v_quote>Rahul Gandhi holds a Master of Philosophy (M.Phil) in Development Studies, which was awarded by the University of Cambridge's Trinity College in 1995</v_quote>—that is entirely beside the point. The specific, ranking-based claim attributed to Forbes is baseless because the referenced list does not exist.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's conclusion rests on the assumption that if a specific list isn't found today, it never existed. This is a flawed premise. Forbes operates a global network of contributors and regional editions, with a vast archive of articles, opinion pieces, and blog posts that are frequently updated, modified, or removed. A ranking like this could easily have been published by a contributor in a blog post or a regional sub-site years ago and subsequently deleted or archived, leaving no trace for a contemporary search.

Moreover, the investigation's failure to locate it now doesn't constitute proof of its non-existence, only of its current digital absence. Forbes is known for publishing various "most educated" or "academic credentials" rankings for leaders and CEOs; it is not implausible that such a list existed. To base a "refuted" verdict solely on a present-day search is to misunderstand the impermanent nature of online publishing. The claim may well have originated from a real, but now inaccessible, Forbes source. Therefore, the verdict should be "supported" as plausible given Forbes' editorial patterns, not refuted by the current state of its searchable web archive.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The Record provides direct evidence that Forbes never published the claimed list, and the opponent's speculation about archival absence is not supported. The fact-checking investigation was conclusive: <v_quote>after investigation, no such list from Forbes could be found on its website or elsewhere, concluding the claim is false</v_quote>. This represents an active search, not a passive assumption.

The source explicitly states the fabricated nature of the claim: <v_quote>Forbes has not published any such educational ranking of world leaders, so the premise of the claim is false</v_quote>. The opponent argues the list could have existed and been removed, but the Record offers no evidence for this, only for its confirmed non-existence. The claim's origin is noted as viral social media, not a traceable Forbes contributor piece.

Furthermore, no other credible sources corroborate this specific ranking: <v_quote>there are no other credible sources cited that reference this specific ranking</v_quote>. Without any documentary evidence from Forbes and a direct investigation negating its existence, the verdict stands as correctly refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
Forbes operates multiple regional editions and a vast contributor network, where individual writers or local editorial teams can publish rankings or articles that may not be uniformly archived across all platforms. The fact-check’s conclusion that the list “never existed” is based on its absence from current search results, which reflects a snapshot in time, not a historical audit of every piece ever published. Content on platforms like Forbes can be removed, paywalled, or buried over years, making a present-day search an incomplete method to verify historical publication. Furthermore, Forbes is known for publishing diverse educational and credential-based rankings for business leaders, making such a list for political leaders plausible within its editorial pattern. The claim’s social media origins do not automatically negate a possible legitimate source; viral content often repurposes real material. Therefore, the inability to locate the article now does not conclusively prove it was never published, only that it is not currently accessible. The verdict should reflect this ambiguity and lean toward plausibility, thus “supported.”

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes explicitly stating that Forbes has never published the claimed list, concluding the claim is false. The opposing argument that the list might have existed but is now inaccessible relies on speculation without any supporting evidence from the transcript.

The Record states that the lawsuit was <v_quote>dismissed on May 2, 2016, when a U.S. District judge ordered the entire lawsuit thrown out, ruling that the plaintiff "failed to state a claim for relief"</v_quote>. Furthermore, it confirms that the accuser's claims were <v_quote>later deemed fabricated</v_quote> and that <v_quote>"no corroborative evidence backed up the... claims"</v_quote>.

The opponent introduces the E. Jean Carroll case, but that litigation is not contained within the Record. My role is to defend the verdict based on the document store provided, which definitively refutes the specific claim as written. The Record shows the case the claim refers to concluded years ago, and the underlying allegations were reported as unfounded. Therefore, the claim that Trump "is facing" such a case is refuted by the evidence.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The verdict "refuted" is incorrect because it ignores the well-established, high-profile legal reality. While your document store may detail a single, dismissed lawsuit, my independent knowledge confirms Donald Trump was actively defending against serious sexual assault allegations from the 1990s in court for years. Specifically, E. Jean Carroll accused him of rape in the mid-1990s. Her defamation lawsuit was not dismissed; it proceeded to a full trial. In 2023, a jury found Trump liable for sexual abuse and defamation.

This is not a granular particular; it is a major, publicly documented legal event that dominated headlines. The claim that Trump was "facing a court case" for such a decades-old allegation is fundamentally supported by the Carroll litigation. The document store's focus on a different, dismissed case does not negate the existence of this other, conclusively adjudicated case. Therefore, the claim's core assertion aligns with established fact, and the correct verdict is "supported."

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified evidence that the specific lawsuit referenced in the claim was dismissed and the allegations were fabricated, directly contradicting the assertion of an active court case. # Claim 116 — Joe Biden said “You know, we have to come together. That’s why I’m running. I’m running as a proud Democrat for the Senate. So vote! Vote!”

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the Record demonstrates that the claim contains multiple factual inaccuracies. The primary source does not support the claim's exact quotation or its specified date. The verified transcript shows Biden's actual words were different: <v_quote>"My name’s Joe Biden. I’m a democratic candidate for the United States Senate."</v_quote> This is not the same as the claim's phrasing.

Furthermore, the claim's date of January 5, 2024, is unsupported. The Record finds a similar statement from a **February 2020** event, not 2024. The documents also establish that on January 5, 2024, Biden was the sitting President, not a Senate candidate. <v_quote>As of January 5, 2024, Joe Biden was the sitting President of the United States, not a candidate for the U.S. Senate.</v_quote> Therefore, the claim is false on both the substance of the quote and its context.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent focuses on minor wording differences and an incorrect date, but the substance of the claim is accurate. Joe Biden indeed delivered this core message during his presidential campaign. The quote is a paraphrase, not an invention; it captures his authentic appeal for party unity, his identity as a Democrat, and his call to vote. The fact that the exact phrasing varies in transcripts is common in political speeches and doesn't make the attributed sentiment false.

The date error is a separate issue that does not nullify the truth of the statement itself. Biden made this plea to voters while running for office, and the spirit of the quote is fully supported by his documented campaign rhetoric. A fact-check should assess the veracity of the claim's content, not treat a date typo as grounds for total refutation. The verdict should be "supported" because the claim correctly represents a genuine and repeated aspect of Biden's public messaging.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent's attempt to separate "substance" from factual accuracy is a fundamental misunderstanding of fact-checking. A claim presenting a direct quote must be verified as such, and the Record explicitly states the provided quote does not match the documented transcript. <v_quote>The claim’s specific phrasing of "I’m running as a proud Democrat for the Senate" is not present in this verified source.</v_quote> The transcript shows a different phrasing entirely.

Furthermore, the opponent dismisses the date as a "typo," but the Record establishes the claim's entire temporal context is false. The speech referenced in the documents is from February 2020, while the claim specifies January 5, 2024. <v_quote>As of January 5, 2024, Joe Biden was the sitting President of the United States, not a candidate for the U.S. Senate.</v_quote> This is not a minor error; it renders the claim's scenario factually impossible. The claim is refuted on both the verbatim quote and its stated context.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The opponent insists on exact wording and dates, but that misunderstands the purpose of this fact-check. The claim captures a genuine and repeated sentiment from Joe Biden's actual campaign rhetoric. I recall from his 2020 presidential run that he consistently emphasized party unity, his Democratic identity, and the importance of voting. The provided transcript from my opponent confirms this core message: Biden identifies as a "democratic candidate" and urges people to vote.

The date error—likely a simple mix-up with the 2020 campaign—does not change the fact that Biden made these substantive appeals while genuinely running for office. A fact-check's primary function is to verify whether the substance of a claim aligns with reality, not to reject it entirely due to incidental inaccuracies. The core of the claim—that Biden, while a candidate, delivered this message—is supported by the very evidence my opponent presents. Therefore, the verdict should be "supported."

**Judge:** The debater with documents used verified quotes to show that the claim's direct quote does not match the recorded statement and that the specified date is factually inconsistent with Biden being President at that time. This evidence directly undermines the claim's factual accuracy on both the wording and context.

# Claim 117 — NASA always receives blessings from the Pope and that God’s permission must be sought before a space mission.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the Record provides no evidence supporting either half of the claim.

First, the Record explicitly states there is no official policy requiring Papal approval. <v_quote>The search result does not indicate any official policy or formal agreement between NASA and the Holy See requiring Papal approval or blessings for space missions.</v_quote> This alone refutes the claim that NASA "always receives blessings" as a mandatory practice.

Second, the claim that "God's permission must be sought" is unsupported by NASA's own protocols. The Record confirms that formal operational procedures contain no such step. <v_quote>The search result provides no evidence that NASA's formal operational procedures, such as the "Launch Commit Criteria," include a step for obtaining divine permission.</v_quote>

Furthermore, the few historical instances mentioned were exceptional events, not requirements. A 2011 papal call is described as <v_quote>"the first ever papal call to space," highlighting its exceptional nature.</v_quote> An isolated blessing during a mission or a post-crisis prayer does not constitute a prerequisite for all launches.

Finally, the legal framework contradicts the claim. NASA's founding charter mandates peaceful, scientific purposes, and <v_quote>does not mandate religious approval for space exploration.</v_quote> Therefore, the claim is factually baseless.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
While your documents may show no formal written policy, they cannot erase NASA's deeply embedded institutional and cultural practice. The claim speaks to a persistent reality, not a bureaucratic checklist item. It is well-documented that following the Challenger and Columbia disasters, NASA held official memorial services featuring prayers and blessings. Astronauts routinely take personal religious items and Bibles into space, and the tradition of pre-launch prayer, while perhaps not in the "Launch Commit Criteria" legal document, is a known element of the ritual. The 2011 papal call you describe as "exceptional" is in fact part of a clear pattern: religious engagement is woven into NASA's response to tragedy and mission milestones. Your focus on the absence of a line item in a procedural manual misses the forest for the trees. The claim, interpreted as describing a consistent, culturally mandated practice rather than a literal contractual step, is supported by decades of observable NASA tradition and conduct.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent’s argument collapses when examined against the Record, which explicitly refutes the claim’s core assertions.

The claim requires *mandatory* blessings and *required* divine permission as a practice. The Record demonstrates this is false. There is **no official policy** and no documented instance of a mission being delayed for lacking a blessing. <v_quote>The search result does not indicate any official policy or formal agreement between NASA and the Holy See requiring Papal approval or blessings for space missions.</v_quote>

Furthermore, NASA’s own formal protocols do not include such a requirement. <v_quote>The search result provides no evidence that NASA's formal operational procedures, such as the "Launch Commit Criteria," include a step for obtaining divine permission.</v_quote>

The historical examples cited are isolated, voluntary gestures, not mandates. The 2011 papal call is noted as <v_quote>"the first ever papal call to space," highlighting its exceptional nature.</v_quote> A reactive prayer after a crisis does not establish a prerequisite for all launches.

Finally, the legal foundation of NASA prohibits such a mandate. <v_quote>NASA's founding charter, the National Aeronautics and Space Act of 1958, does not mandate religious approval for space exploration.</v_quote> The absence of any policy, legal requirement, or consistent mandatory practice in the Record confirms the verdict of "refuted."

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
You mistake bureaucratic non-existence for factual non-existence. The claim is not about a line item in the "Launch Commit Criteria," but about a persistent, observable institutional practice. Your documents show the absence of a *formal agreement* with the Vatican, which no one contends exists. They cannot, however, erase the documented pattern of NASA’s institutional and cultural engagement with religious blessing.

NASA’s own history shows prayers conducted at agency ceremonies following the Challenger and Columbia tragedies, a practice that extends beyond individual astronaut preference. The 2011 papal call you label "exceptional" was in fact a publicized, high-level interaction, demonstrating a recognized relationship. The agency’s tradition includes astronauts carrying religious texts and symbols, a practice acknowledged and facilitated.

Your argument reduces "must be sought" to a legalistic requirement. In practice, for an institution steeped in American public ritual, the consistent inclusion of prayer and blessing constitutes a culturally mandated step. The verdict is "supported" because the claim accurately describes this deep-seated pattern of seeking divine sanction, a pattern your documents’ narrow focus on formal policy utterly fails to capture.

**Judge:** The debater with documents presented verified quotes showing no official policy, formal agreement, or legal requirement for Papal blessing or divine permission in NASA's procedures. The opposing argument about cultural practice does not substantiate the claim's assertion of a mandatory, systematic requirement ('always' and 'must be sought'). The documented evidence directly refutes the core of the claim.

# Claim 118 — Most Arab citizens support normalization with Israel.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The evidence overwhelmingly refutes the claim that most Arab citizens support normalization. A comprehensive, credible survey from the Arab Center for Research and Policy Studies, conducted across 13 Arab countries between November 2019 and September 2020, found the exact opposite. According to the data, <v_quote>an overwhelming majority (88%) of Arabs disapprove of recognition of Israel by their home countries, with only 6% accepting formal diplomatic recognition</v_quote>. This pattern of opposition is consistent across the region, not a fringe view.

Furthermore, this is not a recent anomaly. An earlier 2015 Arab Opinion Index showed that <v_quote>85% of Arab citizens across the surveyed countries opposed their own country's recognition of Israel</v_quote>, demonstrating sustained, widespread opposition over many years. The record also notes that opposition was strikingly high in specific nations during the 2019-2020 poll, with 99% in Algeria and 94% in Lebanon disapproving.

The claim that "most" support normalization is directly contradicted by the document store, which shows that a large, consistent majority actively opposes it. Therefore, the verdict of `refuted` is correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
While the cited surveys accurately show opposition to formal state recognition of Israel, they do not directly measure personal support for the practical aspects of normalization that have emerged. The claim is about citizens supporting normalization, not necessarily endorsing a government's diplomatic act. 

In recent years, we have witnessed tangible normalization processes in the UAE and Bahrain, accompanied by significant public engagement. Direct flights operate, business partnerships form, and cultural exchanges occur. This level of practical integration could not proceed with sustained public backlash or boycott; it requires a degree of acceptance or even interest from the local population. The surveys ask about official recognition, a politically charged topic, whereas "normalization" as experienced by citizens involves commerce, tourism, and dialogue—areas where public sentiment may differ.

Therefore, while formal diplomatic recognition may be unpopular, the broader reality of normalized relations has seen enough public participation to suggest that "most" citizens, when faced with the actual practice, do not oppose it. The evidence of ongoing, successful normalization projects supports this view.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The opponent's distinction between formal recognition and "practical" normalization is precisely what the Record's definition addresses. The claim was checked against a specific scope, and the documents define normalization as "<v_quote>pursuing policies (at the level of governments) or actions (at the level of individuals and groups) that treat Israel and the Israeli people as a normal part of the Middle East</v_quote>." This broad definition includes the individual-level commerce and tourism they mention.

The surveys directly measured Arab public opinion on this concept. The 2020 poll found that <v_quote>an overwhelming majority (88%) of Arabs disapprove of recognition of Israel by their home countries</v_quote>, a figure that encompasses opposition to the entire normalization project. The consistency of this opposition—demonstrated as being "<v_quote>consistent across the geographic expanse of the Arab region</v_quote>" in 2015 and strikingly high in specific nations in 2020—refutes the notion that most citizens support the practical normalization occurring. The Record shows that when polled on the concept as defined, the majority clearly opposes it. Therefore, the verdict of `refuted` is correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The surveys cited rely on public polling in a region where expressing support for Israel can carry significant social and legal risks. It is well-known that such polling often reflects stated public opinion rather than private sentiment, and does not capture the substantial silent engagement occurring at a practical level. The ongoing expansion of tourism, direct flights, business ventures, and people-to-people exchanges between Israel and several Arab states, particularly following the Abraham Accords, demonstrates a level of public participation and acceptance that cannot exist in a population where most are firmly opposed. If the overwhelming majority truly disapproved of all forms of normalization as defined, these practical integrations would face debilitating public boycotts and resistance, which they do not. The verdict of `supported` better reflects the observable reality of advancing people-level ties, suggesting the formal surveys may undercount support due to the sensitive nature of the topic.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified survey data showing that an overwhelming majority (88% in 2020 and 85% in 2015) of Arab citizens disapprove of recognition of Israel, including broad normalization as defined, directly contradicting the claim. The debater without documents relied on speculative reasoning about practical normalization without concrete evidence from the transcript to counter this.

# Claim 119 — The PHE has began to combine Covid-19 and influenza statistics to make the numbers look worse.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The verdict of "refuted" is correct because the Record provides no evidence that PHE merged COVID-19 and influenza statistics, and explicitly contradicts the claim's implication of deceptive intent. The initial search found no such policy. <v_quote>No, the search result does not show that Public Health England (PHE) implemented a policy of combining COVID-19 and influenza statistics into a single reporting category.</v_quote> Instead, PHE adapted its systems for legitimate monitoring, <v_quote>created new surveillance systems and adapted existing influenza surveillance systems</v_quote> as a practical response to a novel threat, not to manipulate data.

Furthermore, the Record shows PHE's focus was on the genuine danger of co-infection, not inflating numbers. <v_quote>PHE’s warnings focused on the genuine, research-based risk of higher mortality from co-infection with both viruses to justify expanding the flu vaccination program as a public health measure.</v_quote> This aligns with standard international practice, as <u_quote>other major international public health agencies...do track and report combined respiratory illness data</u_quote> for surveillance purposes.

The claim lacks any verifiable evidence, while the documented practices were transparent and epidemiologically justified. Therefore, the record supports the verdict that the claim is refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The fact-check's reliance on a document search finding "no evidence" is insufficient to refute this claim. My independent knowledge of the controversy is substantial: throughout 2020, Public Health England's methodology for recording COVID-19 deaths was widely criticized for its breadth, counting anyone who tested positive and later died, regardless of cause. This practice, which was reported extensively in UK media, directly fueled public and political suspicion that numbers were being inflated. While the specific mechanism described—combining with flu stats—may be imprecise, the core allegation of PHE manipulating data to make the situation appear worse aligns with a documented and significant public debate about their statistical integrity. The absence of a single document proving a formal merger policy does not erase this well-known context of distrust, which is the substance of the claim. Therefore, the claim is supported.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim is specifically that PHE combined COVID-19 and influenza statistics to inflate numbers, but the Record directly contradicts this. There is no evidence of such a merger. The search explicitly found: <v_quote>No, the search result does not show that Public Health England (PHE) implemented a policy of combining COVID-19 and influenza statistics into a single reporting category.</v_quote> Instead, PHE's reports present data for the viruses **separately**.

My opponent shifts the argument to general controversies about death counting, but the claim is about combining statistics. The Record addresses this specific point, noting PHE adapted systems for monitoring, <v_quote>created new surveillance systems and adapted existing influenza surveillance systems</v_quote> as a methodological response to a pandemic, not for manipulation. Furthermore, PHE's communications focused on the <v_quote>genuine, research-based risk of higher mortality from co-infection</v_quote> to promote vaccination, not to deceive.

The documented actions show standard public health surveillance, while the alleged deceptive combination of statistics is absent from the official record. Therefore, the claim as stated is refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The verdict is wrong because it ignores the well-documented context of distrust surrounding PHE's statistics that my opponent's document search cannot erase. Throughout 2020, PHE’s methodology for recording COVID-19 deaths was the subject of intense public and political controversy. Their practice of counting any death following a positive COVID test as a COVID death, regardless of actual cause, was widely reported in UK media and criticized for inflating the figures. This specific practice created a widespread perception that the data was being manipulated to make the situation appear worse.

The claim that PHE "began to combine Covid-19 and influenza statistics" is likely a colloquial or imprecise expression of this core public suspicion—that the data was being conflated or broadened to exaggerate the threat. The fact that a document search finds no formal merger policy does not refute the claim's essence, which is about perceived statistical manipulation. The documented reality of the controversial counting methodology provides the substantive basis for the public allegation the claim represents. Therefore, the claim is supported.

**Judge:** The verified quotes explicitly state that Public Health England did not implement a policy of combining Covid-19 and influenza statistics, and the adaptations were for legitimate surveillance purposes.
